1. Study Guide Questions

29 questions

Question 1 of 45
Explain why only one variable can change on the liquid–vapor boundary.
A. Two phases coexist (P = 2), so F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1: once you choose T, P is fixed by the equilibrium line (and vice versa)
B. There is only one component, so F always equals 1
C. On the boundary, three phases coexist, so F = 1
D. Temperature cannot change at all on the boundary

A. Two phases coexist (P = 2), so F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1: once you choose T, P is fixed by the equilibrium line (and vice versa)

On the liquid–vapor boundary, C = 1 and P = 2 → F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1. You can change only one variable freely (either T or P); once you change one, the other is automatically fixed to maintain equilibrium. If you fix the temperature, the pressure is locked by the phase equilibrium line (and vice versa).

To keep both liquid and vapor, you must stay on the line; choosing one coordinate fixes the other.

Question 2 of 45
The labelled water–phenol phase diagram is shown below (phenol in water, % by weight, on the x-axis; temperature on the y-axis). Which choice correctly marks the critical solution temperature (CST) and the tie line?
A. Point a at the bottom left is the CST; the curved boundary of the yellow region is the tie line
B. The yellow region is the CST; point h is the tie line
C. Point h (the top of the curve, 66.8 °C) is the CST; the horizontal line b–c across the yellow two-phase region at 50 °C is the tie line
D. The 70 °C line at the top of the graph is the CST; a vertical line through e is the tie line

C. Point h (the top of the curve, 66.8 °C) is the CST; the horizontal line b–c across the yellow two-phase region at 50 °C is the tie line

Point h, the highest point of the two-phase curve (66.8 °C), is the CST: the highest temperature at which two separate layers can still exist. The tie line is a horizontal line drawn at a fixed temperature across the two-phase (yellow) region, here b–c at 50 °C. Left end (b): composition of the water-rich layer; right end (c): composition of the phenol-rich layer. Above the CST (white area) there is one liquid phase.

CST is the top point h of the curve, and the tie line is the flat line b–c across the two-layer region.

Question 3 of 45
Why does adding more phases reduce the degrees of freedom in the phase rule?
A. P is subtracted in F = C − P + 2, so each extra phase lowers F by one: every additional coexisting phase adds an equilibrium condition that fixes one more variable
B. Because each new phase adds a new component
C. Because more phases increase the temperature
D. Because phases add to the number 2 in the formula

A. P is subtracted in F = C − P + 2, so each extra phase lowers F by one: every additional coexisting phase adds an equilibrium condition that fixes one more variable

In F = C − P + 2 the number of phases is subtracted: for one component, P = 1 → F = 2, P = 2 → F = 1, P = 3 → F = 0. Physically, every extra phase that must stay in equilibrium with the others puts one more restriction on T and P (e.g. to keep liquid and vapor together you must stay on the boiling line), so one fewer variable is free. (Not answered in the study guide.)

Each new phase that must be kept in balance “ties up” one of your knobs.

Question 4 of 45
If you have a beaker of liquid water at 25 °C and 1 atm, how many degrees of freedom (F) does the system have?
A. 0
B. 1
C. 3
D. 2

D. 2

P = 1 (liquid only), C = 1 (H₂O).
F = C − P + 2 = 1 − 1 + 2 = 2 (study guide).

One substance + one phase → F = 2.

Question 5 of 45
Which two variables can you change independently in a one-phase system without changing the number of phases?
A. Temperature and number of components
B. Pressure and number of phases
C. Mass and volume of the container
D. Temperature and pressure

D. Temperature and pressure

You can independently change two variables, usually temperature (T) and pressure (P), without changing the number of phases (study guide).

The two classic “knobs” are T and P.

Question 6 of 45
In a one-phase system, changing the temperature slightly will always produce a new phase.
True
False

False

False. Changing either T or P slightly does not create a new phase until you hit a phase boundary (boiling or freezing line). Example: heat liquid water slightly at constant P → it is still all liquid.

A new phase appears only on the lines, not inside an area.

Question 7 of 45
If F = 0, you can adjust temperature and pressure independently without changing the number of phases.
True
False

False

False. At F = 0 both temperature and pressure are fixed; you cannot change T or P without losing one of the phases. It is fixed at one point only.

F = 0 means zero free knobs.

Question 8 of 45
Boiling water at 100 °C under 1 atm is a two-phase system. What are the two phases present?
A. Ice and water vapor
B. Ice and liquid water
C. Liquid water and water vapor (gas)
D. Water and phenol

C. Liquid water and water vapor (gas)

The two phases are liquid water and water vapor (liquid + gas).

Boiling = liquid turning into vapor, both present together.

Question 9 of 45
Boiling water at 100 °C under 1 atm: calculate F using Gibbs phase rule.
A. 0
B. 3
C. 2
D. 1

D. 1

C = 1, P = 2.
F = C − P + 2 = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1.

Two phases of one substance → one degree of freedom.

Question 10 of 45
Boiling water at 100 °C under 1 atm. If you raise the pressure, what must happen to the temperature to stay on the boiling curve?
A. It must stay at 100 °C
B. It must increase (the boiling point increases)
C. It must decrease
D. It must drop to the triple-point temperature

B. It must increase (the boiling point increases)

If you increase pressure, the boiling point increases: e.g. if you raise P to 2 atm, T must rise to about 120 °C for both phases to coexist.

The boiling line goes up and to the right: more pressure, higher boiling temperature.

Question 11 of 45
Why do we say the triple point is an invariant point?
A. Because all phases have the same composition and density
B. Because it has only one component
C. Because F = 1 − 3 + 2 = 0: neither T nor P can be changed without losing a phase
D. Because the temperature can change but the pressure cannot

C. Because F = 1 − 3 + 2 = 0: neither T nor P can be changed without losing a phase

At the triple point P = 3, C = 1 → F = 1 − 3 + 2 = 0. Invariant = F = 0: no variable can be changed; the system exists only at a specific temperature and pressure (no freedom to change T or P).

In-variant = cannot vary.

Question 12 of 45
If you slightly increase the temperature at the triple point, which phase disappears first?
A. The solid (ice melts), leaving liquid + vapor
B. The liquid, leaving ice + vapor
C. No phase disappears
D. The vapor, leaving ice + liquid

A. The solid (ice melts), leaving liquid + vapor

If you raise temperature slightly → ice melts, leaving only liquid + vapor (2 phases), so we lose the triple point.

Warming melts the ice first.

Question 13 of 45
Define partial miscibility and give one example.
A. Liquids that mix completely in all proportions; e.g. water and ethanol
B. Liquids that never mix; e.g. water and phenol at all temperatures
C. Liquids that mix completely only under certain conditions (specific temperature ranges or compositions) and separate into two layers outside those conditions; e.g. water and phenol
D. A solid that dissolves partly in water; e.g. sugar

C. Liquids that mix completely only under certain conditions (specific temperature ranges or compositions) and separate into two layers outside those conditions; e.g. water and phenol

Partially miscible liquids mix completely only under certain conditions (specific temperature ranges or compositions); outside those conditions they separate into two layers. Example: water and phenol, which at some temperatures and compositions form two separate liquid layers: a water-rich layer and a phenol-rich layer.

Partial = mixes only some of the time.

Question 14 of 45
Describe what a tie line represents in a temperature–composition phase diagram.
A. It shows the pressure at which the two liquids boil
B. It is a vertical line showing how the temperature changes for one composition
C. It is the boundary between the one-phase and two-phase regions
D. It is a horizontal line at a fixed temperature across the two-phase region; its ends give the compositions of the two coexisting layers (left = water-rich, right = phenol-rich)

D. It is a horizontal line at a fixed temperature across the two-phase region; its ends give the compositions of the two coexisting layers (left = water-rich, right = phenol-rich)

A tie line is a horizontal line drawn at a fixed temperature across the two-phase region. Left end: composition of the water-rich layer. Right end: composition of the phenol-rich layer.

The tie line “ties” together the two layers that are in equilibrium.

Question 15 of 45
What happens to the tie line as you approach the critical solution temperature (CST)?
A. It becomes shorter and shrinks to a single point at the CST
B. It becomes longer and reaches the axes
C. It becomes vertical
D. It does not change

A. It becomes shorter and shrinks to a single point at the CST

The two-phase region narrows as the temperature rises, so the tie lines get shorter. At CST (66.8 °C for phenol–water), the tie line shrinks to a single point; above this temperature, water and phenol become completely miscible and form a single layer.

At the top of the dome, both ends of the tie line meet.

Question 16 of 45
Explain why CST is called a “mixing temperature.”
A. Because the two liquids react chemically at this temperature
B. Because below it the liquids are always completely miscible
C. Because if you heat beyond it there is no more separation: everything stays as one liquid phase
D. Because at this temperature the two liquids boil together

C. Because if you heat beyond it there is no more separation: everything stays as one liquid phase

Think of CST as a “mixing temperature”: if you heat beyond this point, there is no more separation; everything stays as one liquid phase (water and phenol become completely miscible and form a single layer).

Above the CST, phenol and water always mix.

Question 17 of 45
Calculate F for a one-phase, one-component system (liquid water).
A. 0
B. 3
C. 2
D. 1

C. 2

C = 1, P = 1.
F = C − P + 2 = 1 − 1 + 2 = 2.

One and one cancel, leaving 2.

Question 18 of 45
Calculate F for a two-phase, one-component system (liquid + vapor).
A. 1
B. 3
C. 2
D. 0

A. 1

C = 1, P = 2.
F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1.

One component, two phases: one freedom.

Question 19 of 45
“A ___ is a physically distinct, homogeneous part of a system separated by a boundary.”
A. phase
B. component
C. tie line
D. degree of freedom

A. phase

A phase is a physically distinct, homogeneous part of a system, separated from other portions by bounding surfaces (study guide: “a visibly distinct part of a system”).

Phases have walls (boundaries) between them.

Question 20 of 45
“The smallest number of chemically independent substances needed to describe a system is called a ___.”
A. critical solution temperature
B. phase
C. degree of freedom
D. component

D. component

This is the definition of a component (C): the smallest number of chemically independent substances needed to describe the composition of the phases.

Components are the chemical “ingredients” of the system.

Question 21 of 45
“Gibbs Phase Rule is written as: F = __ – __ + 2.”
A. P; C
B. C; P
C. P; F
D. C; F

B. C; P

F = C − P + 2: degrees of freedom = number of components − number of phases + 2.

Remember the order: freedom equals components minus phases plus two.

Question 22 of 45
“A one-phase, one-component system has __ degrees of freedom.”
A. 3
B. 2
C. 0
D. 1

B. 2

F = 1 − 1 + 2 = 2 degrees of freedom (bivariant).

One phase, one component → two.

Question 23 of 45
“In a one-phase system, we can change both __ and __ independently.”
A. pressure; number of components
B. temperature; pressure
C. composition; number of phases
D. temperature; number of phases

B. temperature; pressure

In a one-phase system we can change both temperature and pressure independently (F = 2).

T and P are the two free knobs.

Question 24 of 45
“In a two-phase, one-component system, the degrees of freedom are __.”
A. 3
B. 2
C. 1
D. 0

C. 1

F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1 (univariant).

Two phases → one freedom.

Question 25 of 45
“At the triple point, the degrees of freedom are __.”
A. 2
B. 3
C. 0
D. 1

C. 0

C = 1 (H₂O), P = 3 (solid + liquid + gas).
F = 1 − 3 + 2 = 0 (invariant).

Three phases of one substance → zero freedom.

Question 26 of 45
“The highest temperature at which phase separation occurs in a partially miscible system is called the ____.”
A. boiling point
B. critical solution temperature (CST)
C. triple point
D. tie line

B. critical solution temperature (CST)

The critical solution temperature (CST) is the highest temperature at which two separate layers can still exist.

CST = the ceiling of the two-layer region.

Question 27 of 45
“A ___ line is a horizontal line that connects the compositions of two coexisting phases at a given temperature in a binary phase diagram.”
A. boiling
B. tie
C. critical
D. boundary

B. tie

A tie line is a horizontal line that connects the compositions of the two coexisting phases at a given temperature.

It ties the two layers together.

Question 28 of 45
“Above the CST, phenol and water are completely ____.”
A. miscible
B. evaporated
C. frozen
D. immiscible

A. miscible

Above the CST, phenol and water become completely miscible and form a single layer.

Above the CST → always mixed.

Question 29 of 45
“In a two-phase system, temperature and pressure are ___ (independent / dependent) on each other.”
A. Dependent: when one is changed, the other is fixed by the equilibrium line
B. Independent: both can be changed freely
C. Both permanently fixed: neither can change
D. Unrelated: pressure has no effect on the phases

A. Dependent: when one is changed, the other is fixed by the equilibrium line

In a two-phase system they are dependent: only one variable can be changed; the other is automatically fixed by equilibrium (dependent).

On the line, T and P move together like linked gears.

2. Phase Rule (Gibbs Rule)

1 question

Question 30 of 45
The phase rule (Gibbs rule) defines:
A. The rate at which a system reaches equilibrium
B. The number of variables (like temperature or pressure) that can be changed without changing the equilibrium state of the system
C. The total mass of all the phases in a system
D. The number of chemical reactions that can occur in a system

B. The number of variables (like temperature or pressure) that can be changed without changing the equilibrium state of the system

Phase Rule (Gibbs Rule) → defines the number of variables (like temperature or pressure) that can be changed without changing the equilibrium state of the system.

It counts your “free knobs”: how many conditions you can turn while the system stays the same.

3. Difference Between Phase & Component

1 question

Question 31 of 45
A glass of ice water (ice cubes in liquid water) has:
A. 2 components and 2 phases
B. 1 component and 1 phase
C. 1 component and 2 phases
D. 2 components and 1 phase

C. 1 component and 2 phases

Study guide: a glass of ice water has two phases: ice (solid) and water (liquid). Both are H₂O → 1 component.

Ice and water are the same chemical in two states.

4. One-Phase System: Liquid Water

1 question

Question 32 of 45
Starting from liquid water at 25 °C and 1 atm, which change keeps the system as a single liquid phase?
A. Lowering P to the triple-point value and T to 0.01 °C
B. Lowering T to −20 °C at 1 atm
C. Raising T to 150 °C at 1 atm
D. Raising T to 30 °C and P to 1.2 atm at the same time

D. Raising T to 30 °C and P to 1.2 atm at the same time

In a one-phase region both T and P can be varied together, as long as the system stays away from the boiling and freezing lines. 30 °C / 1.2 atm is still well inside the liquid region. −20 °C at 1 atm is solid, 150 °C at 1 atm is gas, and the triple point has three phases.

Small changes in the middle of the liquid area keep you liquid.

5. Two-Phase System: Liquid Water and Vapor

1 question

Question 33 of 45
At 2 atm, for liquid water and vapor to coexist, the temperature must rise to about:
A. 80 °C
B. 100 °C
C. 0.01 °C
D. 120 °C

D. 120 °C

At 2 atm, T must rise to about 120 °C for both phases to coexist.

Higher pressure → higher boiling point (like a pressure cooker).

6. Triple Point of Water

2 questions

Question 34 of 45
The triple point of water is at:
A. 0.01 °C and 0.006 atm
B. 100 °C and 1 atm
C. 0 °C and 1 atm
D. 374 °C and 218 atm

A. 0.01 °C and 0.006 atm

At the triple point (dot), 0.01 °C and 0.006 atm: ice, liquid and vapor coexist. (Note: another diagram in the lecture writes 0.0098 °C and 6.0 × 10⁻³ atm; the lecture text and study guide use 0.01 °C and 0.006 atm.)

Just above freezing, at a very low pressure.

Question 35 of 45
At the triple point, the pressure is lowered slightly. What happens?
A. Nothing changes; three phases remain
B. Liquid evaporates, leaving ice + vapor
C. Vapor condenses, leaving ice + liquid
D. Ice melts, leaving liquid + vapor

B. Liquid evaporates, leaving ice + vapor

Lower P slightly: liquid evaporates, leaving ice + vapor. Only two phases remain, so the system is no longer at the triple point.

Lower pressure favors the vapor; the liquid is lost.

7. Phase Diagram of Water & Degrees of Freedom Table

2 questions

Question 36 of 45
Gas–liquid, liquid–solid, or gas–solid systems (the boundaries) of a single component are:
A. Bivariant (F = 2): both variables are independent
B. Invariant (F = 0)
C. Univariant only for gas–liquid, but bivariant for liquid–solid
D. Univariant (F = 1): only one variable can be changed; the other is automatically fixed by equilibrium

D. Univariant (F = 1): only one variable can be changed; the other is automatically fixed by equilibrium

Two phases: F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1 → univariant. Only one variable can be changed. The other is automatically fixed by equilibrium (dependent). This applies to all three boundaries.

SystemGas–liquid, liquid–solid, or gas–solid (boundaries)
Number of phases2
Degree of freedomF = C − P + 2 = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1
CommentsSystem is univariant (F = 1). Only one variable can be changed. The other is automatically fixed by equilibrium (dependent).

Every boundary line has one freedom.

Question 37 of 45
In the phase diagram of water below, what are the degrees of freedom at points G, B and T, in that order?
A. 2, 1, 0
B. 2, 1, 1
C. 1, 1, 0
D. 2, 2, 0

A. 2, 1, 0

G lies inside the gas region (one phase): F = 1 − 1 + 2 = 2 (bivariant). B is on the liquid–gas curve (two phases): F = 1 − 2 + 2 = 1 (univariant). T is the triple point (three phases): F = 1 − 3 + 2 = 0 (invariant). So 2, 1, 0.

Area → 2, line → 1, point where three lines meet → 0.

8. Water–Phenol System: Partial Miscibility & CST

3 questions

Question 38 of 45
In the water–phenol system, miscibility depends on:
A. Composition only; temperature has no effect
B. The color of the container
C. Pressure only
D. Composition and temperature (pressure is constant)

D. Composition and temperature (pressure is constant)

Miscibility depends on composition and temperature (pressure here is constant).

The diagram axes are exactly these two variables: composition (x) and temperature (y).

Question 39 of 45
A 50% phenol–water mixture shows two layers at 25 °C. To obtain a single clear liquid by heating alone, it must be heated above about:
A. 100 °C
B. 66.8 °C
C. 25 °C
D. 50 °C

B. 66.8 °C

Above the CST (66.8 °C) all combinations of phenol and water are completely miscible. Below it, a 50% mixture lies inside the two-phase dome.

Clear the top of the dome to get one phase.

Question 40 of 45
A 40% phenol mixture is one clear phase at 75 °C. It is then cooled to 40 °C. What is observed?
A. It forms three liquid layers
B. It freezes completely
C. It stays as one clear liquid
D. It separates into two liquid layers

D. It separates into two liquid layers

At 75 °C (above the CST of 66.8 °C) the mixture is one phase. At 40 °C, 40% phenol lies inside the two-phase (yellow) region (the boundary at 40 °C is at about 9% and about 67% phenol on the diagram), so the mixture separates into a water-rich and a phenol-rich layer.

Going back under the dome brings the two layers back.

9. Tie Line, Test Tube Experiment & Other Two-Component Systems

5 questions

Question 41 of 45
In the test tube experiment, tube 8 contains 6.5 g phenol and 3.5 g water. What is its phenol concentration (% w/w)?
Test tube #12345678910
Phenol (g)0.511.524566.578
Water (g)9.598.586543.532
A. 35%
B. 6.5%
C. 65%
D. 185.7%

C. 65%

Conc. of phenol (% w/w) = mass of phenol ÷ total mass × 100
= 6.5 ÷ (6.5 + 3.5) × 100 = 6.5 ÷ 10 × 100 = 65%.

Always divide by the total mass of the mixture, not by the water alone.

Question 42 of 45
In the test tube experiment (table below), the first and second concentrations of the tie line were taken as:
Test tube #12345678910
Phenol (% w/w)5101520405060657080
Two-phase appearance−++++++++−
A. 5% and 80% phenol (tubes 1 and 10)
B. 20% and 60% phenol (tubes 4 and 7)
C. 10% and 70% phenol (tubes 2 and 9)
D. 40% and 50% phenol (tubes 5 and 6)

C. 10% and 70% phenol (tubes 2 and 9)

The lecture’s table marks tube 2 (10%) as the 1st conc. of the tie line and tube 9 (70%) as the 2nd conc. of the tie line: the first and last tubes that showed two phases at that temperature. (In the figure, these two tubes are circled and highlighted in yellow.)

The tie line ends are at the edges of the “+” range.

Question 43 of 45
A 40% phenol mixture is kept at 50 °C and separates into two layers. Using the diagram below, what are the compositions of the two layers?
A. About 11% phenol (water-rich layer) and about 63% phenol (phenol-rich layer)
B. About 0% and 100% phenol
C. About 40% and 60% phenol
D. Both layers contain 40% phenol

A. About 11% phenol (water-rich layer) and about 63% phenol (phenol-rich layer)

Any mixture inside the two-phase region at 50 °C separates into the two phases at the ends of the 50 °C tie line: b ≈ 11% phenol (water-rich) and c ≈ 63% phenol (phenol-rich). The overall 40% composition is not the composition of either layer.

Read the ends of the tie line, not the starting point.

Question 44 of 45
On the lecture’s water–phenol diagram, three mixtures with different overall phenol contents (labelled d, e and f) lie on the same 50 °C tie line, between its ends b (11% phenol) and c (63% phenol). Which statement is correct?
A. Each mixture gives layers of a different composition
B. All three separate into the same two layers (about 11% and 63% phenol); only the relative amounts of the two layers differ
C. Mixture e forms one phase because it is in the middle
D. The layers of d contain more phenol than the layers of f

B. All three separate into the same two layers (about 11% and 63% phenol); only the relative amounts of the two layers differ

All points on one tie line separate into the two phases at its ends (b = 11%, c = 63% phenol). What changes from b to c is how much of each layer forms: in the lecture’s tube drawings the phenol-rich layer gets larger and the water-rich layer smaller as the overall phenol content rises from d to f. (In the figure, the hatched part of each tube is the water-rich layer and the white part is the phenol-rich layer.)

Same tie line → same two layers; only their volumes change.

Question 45 of 45
Which statement correctly compares the three two-component systems in the lecture?
A. Phenol–water separates only at high temperatures
B. All three separate only at low temperatures
C. All three are completely miscible at all temperatures
D. Phenol–water separates below 66.8 °C; triethylamine–water separates at higher temperatures; nicotine–water separates only between about 60.8 °C and 208 °C

D. Phenol–water separates below 66.8 °C; triethylamine–water separates at higher temperatures; nicotine–water separates only between about 60.8 °C and 208 °C

Phenol–water: two phases under the dome, one phase above the CST (66.8 °C). Triethylamine–water: one phase at low temperature, two phases above about 18–20 °C. Nicotine–water: two phases only inside a closed loop between 60.8 °C and 208 °C. (Figures below, in that order: phenol–water, triethylamine–water, nicotine–water.)

Same idea, partial miscibility, but the shape of the two-phase region differs.

Lecture 12Eutectic Mixtures

45 questions

1. Study Guide Questions2. Eutectic Mixture: Definition & Why the Melting Point Is Lower3. Examples: Deep Eutectic Solvent & EMLA Cream4. Phase Diagram of Two Solids (A & B): Axes & Regions5. Eutectic Point, Eutectic Temperature & Eutectic Composition6. Phase Diagram of the EMLA Eutectic Mixture7. Solid Dispersions: Types, Mechanism & Benefits8. Eutectic Mixtures in Daily Life9. Menthol–Camphor Eutectic System

1. Study Guide Questions

17 questions

Question 1 of 45
How many phases exist at the eutectic point? What are they?
A. Two: solid A and solid B
B. Three: solid, liquid and gas
C. Two: liquid A and liquid B
D. Three: liquid (homogeneous A + B mixture), solid A and solid B

D. Three: liquid (homogeneous A + B mixture), solid A and solid B

At the eutectic point, three phases coexist simultaneously: the homogeneous liquid mixture (A + B), solid A and solid B. (Not answered in the study guide.)

One liquid plus two different kinds of crystals.

Question 2 of 45
Why do eutectic mixtures melt at a lower temperature than either pure component?
A. One component evaporates and carries heat away from the other
B. The molecules of one component disrupt the crystal packing of the other; this weakens the lattice, so less energy (a lower temperature) is needed to turn the mixture into a liquid
C. Mixing increases the size of the crystals, so they melt sooner
D. The two components form a new compound with stronger bonds that break more easily

B. The molecules of one component disrupt the crystal packing of the other; this weakens the lattice, so less energy (a lower temperature) is needed to turn the mixture into a liquid

In a pure solid, molecules are arranged in a stable, organised crystal packing that needs a certain amount of energy (heat) to break apart. When two solids are mixed in the right proportion, the molecules of one component disrupt the crystal packing of the other. This interference weakens the lattice, so less energy (lower temperature) is needed to convert the mixture into a liquid. (Not answered in the study guide.)

A messy stack of mixed blocks topples more easily than a neat stack of identical blocks.

Question 3 of 45
In the eutectic phase diagram of A and B below, which labelling of point P and regions W, X, Y and Z is correct?
A. P = eutectic point; W = (ii) solid A + solid B; X = (iv) solid B + liquid; Y = (iii) solid A + liquid; Z = (i) liquid A + B
B. P = melting point of A; W = (i) liquid A + B; X = (iv) solid B + liquid; Y = (iii) solid A + liquid; Z = (ii) solid A + solid B
C. P = melting point of B; W = (iii) solid A + liquid; X = (i) liquid A + B; Y = (ii) solid A + solid B; Z = (iv) solid B + liquid
D. P = eutectic point; W = (i) liquid A + B; X = (iii) solid A + liquid; Y = (iv) solid B + liquid; Z = (ii) solid A + solid B

D. P = eutectic point; W = (i) liquid A + B; X = (iii) solid A + liquid; Y = (iv) solid B + liquid; Z = (ii) solid A + solid B

A correct diagram: y‑axis temperature, x‑axis composition (100% A on the left → 100% B on the right). Two curves fall from m.p. A and m.p. B and meet at the lowest point, the eutectic point (P = E), which lies on the horizontal eutectic‑temperature line.
• W (above the curves) = (i) homogeneous liquid of A + B
• X (A side, between curve and line) = (iii) crystals of pure A + liquid mixture
• Y (B side) = (iv) crystals of pure B + liquid mixture
• Z (below the eutectic line) = (ii) solid A + solid B (Not answered in the study guide.)

Top = liquid, bottom = solid, left wedge = extra A solid, right wedge = extra B solid.

Question 4 of 45
Explain the pharmaceutical advantage of preparing a drug as a eutectic mixture.
A. It raises the drug's melting point so the tablet does not melt in the mouth
B. The eutectic has a lower melting point (it can be a liquid that is easy to process or make into creams), and the drug ends up as very fine microcrystals with a large surface area that dissolve faster, improving absorption and bioavailability
C. It turns the drug into large, organised crystals that dissolve more slowly for a longer effect
D. It chemically changes the drug into a new, more potent compound

B. The eutectic has a lower melting point (it can be a liquid that is easy to process or make into creams), and the drug ends up as very fine microcrystals with a large surface area that dissolve faster, improving absorption and bioavailability

Advantages: (1) lower melting point → easier to melt, mix and process; at room temperature the mixture may already be liquid, which is easy to formulate into creams/gels (e.g. EMLA). (2) As a solid dispersion, the drug is distributed as very fine microcrystals → ↑ surface area → faster dissolution → better absorption → improved bioavailability and better treatment. (Not answered in the study guide.)

Lower melting point for easier formulation; tiny crystals for faster dissolving.

Question 5 of 45
Why does griseofulvin need to be mixed with succinic acid?
A. Succinic acid reacts with griseofulvin to form a new antibiotic
B. Succinic acid raises the melting point of griseofulvin so tablets are harder
C. Griseofulvin is poorly water‑soluble (large, organised crystals); forming a eutectic solid dispersion with the water‑soluble carrier succinic acid leaves it as tiny microcrystals with a larger surface area, so it dissolves faster and its absorption and bioavailability improve
D. Succinic acid makes griseofulvin more crystalline and slower to dissolve

C. Griseofulvin is poorly water‑soluble (large, organised crystals); forming a eutectic solid dispersion with the water‑soluble carrier succinic acid leaves it as tiny microcrystals with a larger surface area, so it dissolves faster and its absorption and bioavailability improve

Griseofulvin is a poorly soluble drug (crystalline, organised crystals). Mixed with the carrier succinic acid it forms a eutectic mixture (lower melting point than drug or carrier). In water/GI fluid the carrier dissolves quickly → drug left in a finely divided state (tiny microcrystals) → increased surface area → faster dissolution → better absorption → improved bioavailability → better treatment. (Not answered in the study guide.)

Succinic acid is the fast‑dissolving helper that breaks griseofulvin into tiny pieces.

Question 6 of 45
“A eutectic mixture is a combination of two __ substances that melt at a __ temperature than either of the pure substances.”
A. solid; higher
B. gaseous; lower
C. solid; lower
D. liquid; lower

C. solid; lower

A eutectic mixture is a combination of two solid substances that melt at a lower temperature than either of the pure substances. (Not answered in the study guide.)

Two solids in, one liquid out — at a lower temperature.

Question 7 of 45
“The eutectic composition is the specific __ of A and B that gives the __ melting point.”
A. ratio; lowest
B. ratio; highest
C. pressure; lowest
D. temperature; lowest

A. ratio; lowest

The eutectic composition is the specific ratio of A and B that gives the lowest melting point. (Not answered in the study guide.)

Composition = ratio; eutectic = lowest.

Question 8 of 45
“Below the eutectic temperature, a mixture forms very fine __ of A and B.”
A. crystals
B. amorphous glasses
C. liquid droplets
D. gas bubbles

A. crystals

Below the eutectic temperature, a mixture forms very fine crystals of A and B (a solid eutectic mixture). (Not answered in the study guide.)

Freezing makes crystals — here, very tiny ones.

Question 9 of 45
“EMLA cream is an example of a __ mixture containing __ and __.”
A. azeotropic; lidocaine; prilocaine
B. solid solution; benzocaine; prilocaine
C. eutectic; lidocaine; prilocaine
D. eutectic; lidocaine; procaine

C. eutectic; lidocaine; prilocaine

EMLA cream is an example of a eutectic mixture containing lidocaine and prilocaine (EMLA = Eutectic Mixture of Local Anaesthetics). (Not answered in the study guide.)

EMLA stands for Eutectic Mixture of Local Anaesthetics.

Question 10 of 45
“Microcrystalline particles dissolve faster because they have increased __ __.”
A. surface area
B. lattice energy
C. crystal size
D. melting point

A. surface area

Microcrystalline particles dissolve faster because they have increased surface area. (Not answered in the study guide.)

More surface touching the water → faster dissolving.

Question 11 of 45
“Eutectic temperature is the lowest temperature at which __ phase exists.”
A. liquid
B. amorphous
C. solid
D. gas

A. liquid

Eutectic temperature is the lowest temperature at which liquid phase exists. (Not answered in the study guide.)

Below it there is no liquid at all.

Question 12 of 45
Which of the following is TRUE about eutectic mixtures?
A. They melt at a higher temperature than either of the components
B. They melt at a lower temperature than either of the components
C. They are always amorphous
D. They form only one solid phase below the eutectic temperature

B. They melt at a lower temperature than either of the components

A eutectic mixture exhibits a lower melting point than either of the pure components: the molecules of one component disrupt the crystal packing of the other, so less energy (lower temperature) is needed to melt it. It is not amorphous: below the eutectic temperature the mixture solidifies as two solids, A and B, as very fine (microcrystalline) crystals, so there are two solid phases, not one.

A eutectic mixture melts lower than both of its pure components.

Question 13 of 45
At the eutectic point:
A. Only solid A and solid B coexist
B. Only liquid A and liquid B coexist
C. Liquid + solid A + solid B coexist
D. None of the above

C. Liquid + solid A + solid B coexist

At the eutectic point (E), three phases coexist simultaneously: the liquid homogeneous mixture (A+B), solid A and solid B. This is similar to the triple point of water, but with solid A, solid B and liquid mixture instead of solid, liquid and gas.

At point E, the liquid and both solids exist together (3 phases).

Question 14 of 45
The eutectic composition is:
A. The ratio giving the highest melting point
B. Any random mixture of A and B
C. The ratio giving the lowest melting point
D. The point where only solid A exists

C. The ratio giving the lowest melting point

The eutectic composition is the exact ratio of A and B that gives the minimum (lowest) melting temperature. The specific ratio is critical: not every mixture of two solids is a eutectic, and all other ratios melt over a range of temperatures.

Eutectic composition = the one ratio of A and B with the lowest melting point.

Question 15 of 45
Which is NOT a type of solid dispersion?
A. Eutectic mixture
B. Solid solution
C. Amorphous dispersion
D. Simple suspension

D. Simple suspension

The types of solid dispersions are: eutectic mixture (drug + carrier crystallize separately but in a very fine microcrystalline form), solid solution (drug completely dissolved in the carrier as a single-phase solid) and amorphous dispersion (drug in a disordered, non-crystalline form). A simple suspension is not one of them.

The three solid dispersions are eutectic mixture, solid solution and amorphous dispersion; a suspension is not one.

Question 16 of 45
The main reason microcrystals dissolve faster is:
A. Lower bioavailability
B. Higher melting point
C. Larger surface area
D. Poor wettability

C. Larger surface area

Surface area effect: smaller particles → more surface area → faster dissolution. (Hydrophilic carriers also increase wettability, so water reaches the drug surface more easily; faster dissolution leads to better, not lower, bioavailability.)

Tiny crystals have much more surface area, so they dissolve faster.

Question 17 of 45
Which of the following about the eutectic mixture of lidocaine and prilocaine (EMLA cream) is FALSE?
A. It contains lidocaine and prilocaine
B. Its melting point is higher than that of pure lidocaine
C. It forms a liquid at room temperature
D. It is emulsified with water to make a cream

B. Its melting point is higher than that of pure lidocaine

FALSE: its melting point is not higher than pure lidocaine. Pure lidocaine melts at 68 °C and pure prilocaine at 38 °C; mixed 1:1 by weight the melting point drops to ~18 °C, so at room temperature (around 25 °C) the mixture is already a liquid. This liquid eutectic is emulsified (dispersed) in water to form a smooth, homogeneous cream. The other statements are true.

EMLA melts at about 18 °C, much lower than lidocaine (68 °C), so B is false.

2. Eutectic Mixture: Definition & Why the Melting Point Is Lower

3 questions

Question 18 of 45
In the lecture's scheme “SOLID + SOLID → MIX → LIQUID”, which two conditions are written under the arrow and the solids?
A. Any ratio of the two solids and room temperature only
B. A high pressure and a catalyst
C. A specific ratio of the two solids and a specific temperature
D. Addition of water and vigorous stirring

C. A specific ratio of the two solids and a specific temperature

The scheme shows: SOLID + SOLID (specific ratio) → MIX at a specific temperature → LIQUID.

The recipe needs the right amounts and the right temperature.

Question 19 of 45
In the lecture's fat example, which pair of fats forms a eutectic mixture, and at what composition?
A. TS (mono acid saturated fat) + CB (monounsaturated fat), at 65 wt% TS + 35 wt% CB
B. TS + CB, at 50 wt% TS + 50 wt% CB
C. CO (mixed medium‑chain saturated fat) + CB (monounsaturated fat), at 65 wt% CO + 35 wt% CB
D. CO + CB, at 35 wt% CO + 65 wt% CB

C. CO (mixed medium‑chain saturated fat) + CB (monounsaturated fat), at 65 wt% CO + 35 wt% CB

The lecture’s figure shows: TS + CB → phase separation in solid state; CB + CO → forming eutectic mixture at CO 65 wt% + CB 35 wt%.

Only the CO/CB pair mixes its crystals together; TS and CB stay apart.

Question 20 of 45
When a eutectic‑composition mixture reaches its eutectic temperature, what happens?
A. It completely turns into a single liquid phase with both components uniformly mixed
B. It separates into two liquid layers
C. It sublimes directly into vapour
D. It only partially melts, leaving crystals of the higher‑melting component

A. It completely turns into a single liquid phase with both components uniformly mixed

When the mixture reaches its eutectic temperature it does not just partially melt — it completely turns into a single liquid phase containing both components uniformly mixed.

At the eutectic point the whole sample turns to liquid at once.

3. Examples: Deep Eutectic Solvent & EMLA Cream

3 questions

Question 21 of 45
In the lecture's deep eutectic solvent example, choline chloride (m.p. ≈ 300 °C) is mixed with urea (m.p. ≈ 130 °C). What is the approximate eutectic temperature of the mixture?
A. 300 °C
B. 130 °C
C. 215 °C
D. 12 °C

D. 12 °C

Choline chloride (≈300 °C) + urea (≈130 °C) → deep eutectic mixture with eutectic temperature ≈12 °C — far below both. (215 °C is just the average — a common mistake.)

Two very high‑melting solids become a liquid near room temperature.

Question 22 of 45
EMLA cream is used as:
A. A systemic antifungal tablet
B. A local anaesthetic before minor procedures
C. A sunscreen
D. An oral antibiotic

B. A local anaesthetic before minor procedures

Lecture: EMLA cream (lidocaine + prilocaine) is used as a local anaesthetic before minor procedures.

EMLA numbs the skin before a needle.

Question 23 of 45
What are the melting points of pure lidocaine and pure prilocaine?
A. Lidocaine 18 °C; prilocaine 38 °C
B. Lidocaine 68 °C; prilocaine 38 °C
C. Lidocaine 68 °C; prilocaine 18 °C
D. Lidocaine 38 °C; prilocaine 68 °C

B. Lidocaine 68 °C; prilocaine 38 °C

Pure lidocaine alone → m.p. 68 °C; pure prilocaine alone → m.p. 38 °C.

Lidocaine is the higher one (68), prilocaine the lower (38).

4. Phase Diagram of Two Solids (A & B): Axes & Regions

3 questions

Question 24 of 45
What are the two variables plotted on the phase diagram of two solids A and B?
A. Temperature on the y‑axis and composition on the x‑axis
B. Pressure on the y‑axis and volume on the x‑axis
C. Composition on the y‑axis and time on the x‑axis
D. Pressure on the y‑axis and temperature on the x‑axis

A. Temperature on the y‑axis and composition on the x‑axis

The phase diagram has two variables: temperature (y‑axis) and composition (x‑axis).

Up = hotter; sideways = more B.

Question 25 of 45
In the phase diagram below, which phases are present at point K?
A. Solid A + solid B only
B. Crystals of pure A + liquid mixture of A and B
C. Homogeneous liquid only
D. Crystals of pure B + liquid mixture of A and B

B. Crystals of pure A + liquid mixture of A and B

Point K: 20% B, 80 °C. The A‑side curve at 20% B is at 140 − (100/60)×20 ≈ 106.7 °C. K (80 °C) lies below that curve but above the eutectic line (40 °C), on the A side → region (iii): crystals of pure A + liquid mixture.

Below the curve but above the line, on the A side = A crystals in liquid.

Question 26 of 45
In the same diagram, which phases are present at point L (85% B, 60 °C)?
A. Solid A + solid B only
B. Crystals of pure A + liquid mixture of A and B
C. Homogeneous liquid only
D. Crystals of pure B + liquid mixture of A and B

D. Crystals of pure B + liquid mixture of A and B

The B‑side curve runs from Q (60% B, 40 °C) to m.p. B (100% B, 100 °C). At 85% B it is at 40 + 60×(85 − 60)/40 = 77.5 °C. L (60 °C) is below this curve and above 40 °C on the B side → region (iv): crystals of B + liquid.

On the B side, under the curve, the extra B stays as crystals.

5. Eutectic Point, Eutectic Temperature & Eutectic Composition

4 questions

Question 27 of 45
In the phase diagram below, what is the eutectic composition?
A. 60% B (40% A)
B. 30% B (70% A)
C. 40% B (60% A)
D. 100% B

A. 60% B (40% A)

The eutectic composition is the x‑value of the eutectic point Q, where the two curves meet: 60% B (so 40% A). 40 is the eutectic temperature (°C), not the composition.

Drop straight down from the bottom of the V to the x‑axis.

Question 28 of 45
In the phase diagram below, what is the eutectic temperature?
A. 100 °C
B. 40 °C
C. 140 °C
D. 60 °C

B. 40 °C

The eutectic temperature is the temperature of the horizontal line through the eutectic point Q: 40 °C. 60 is the eutectic composition (% B); 100 and 140 °C are the melting points of pure B and pure A.

Read the height of the flat line.

Question 29 of 45
A mixture of A and B that is NOT at the eutectic composition is heated from below the eutectic temperature. How does it melt?
A. Over a range of temperatures, not sharply at a single temperature
B. It cannot melt at all
C. Sharply at the eutectic temperature, exactly like the eutectic composition
D. Sharply at the melting point of the higher‑melting component

A. Over a range of temperatures, not sharply at a single temperature

Only the eutectic composition melts (sharply) at the eutectic temperature — all other ratios melt over a range of temperatures.

Only the special recipe melts all at once.

Question 30 of 45
A eutectic‑composition mixture is studied at three temperatures. Which match is correct?
A. Above: solid A + liquid; at it: solid B + liquid; below: fully liquid
B. Above the eutectic temperature: fully liquid; exactly at it: freezes/melts sharply; below it: very fine crystalline solid mixture of A and B
C. Above: fine crystals; at it: fully liquid; below: amorphous glass
D. Above: two liquid layers; at it: one solid; below: gas

B. Above the eutectic temperature: fully liquid; exactly at it: freezes/melts sharply; below it: very fine crystalline solid mixture of A and B

Eutectic temperature: above this temperature → fully liquid; exactly at this temperature → freezes/melts sharply; below this → a very fine, crystalline solid mixture of A and B.

Like pure ice at 0 °C, the eutectic mixture melts sharply at one temperature.

6. Phase Diagram of the EMLA Eutectic Mixture

3 questions

Question 31 of 45
In the lecture's phase diagram of the lidocaine–prilocaine (EMLA) system, the eutectic point lies at:
A. 50% (1:1 by weight) and 18 °C
B. 100% prilocaine and 38 °C
C. 50% and 38 °C
D. 0% prilocaine and 68 °C

A. 50% (1:1 by weight) and 18 °C

The diagram marks E at 1:1 by weight (50 on the composition axis) with the eutectic temperature = 18 °C labelled “lowest melting point”. The curves start at m.p. lidocaine = 68 °C (left, 100% lidocaine) and m.p. prilocaine = 38 °C (right, 100% prilocaine).

The bottom of the V is at half‑and‑half, 18 °C.

Question 32 of 45
In the same diagram, what is the state at point U (50% prilocaine, 10 °C)?
A. Crystals of lidocaine + liquid
B. Crystals of prilocaine + liquid
C. Homogeneous liquid mixture
D. Solid lidocaine + solid prilocaine

D. Solid lidocaine + solid prilocaine

U is at 10 °C, below the eutectic temperature (18 °C) → region of solid lidocaine + prilocaine (fine crystals of both).

Cooler than 18 °C → even the eutectic mixture freezes.

Question 33 of 45
A pharmacist accidentally mixes lidocaine and prilocaine 9:1 by weight. Compared with the 1:1 mixture, at 25 °C this mixture will:
A. Be fully liquid, like the 1:1 mixture
B. Melt at a lower temperature than 18 °C
C. Be fully solid prilocaine
D. Still contain lidocaine crystals, because only the 1:1 ratio gives the lowest melting point (18 °C)

D. Still contain lidocaine crystals, because only the 1:1 ratio gives the lowest melting point (18 °C)

9:1 lidocaine:prilocaine = 10% prilocaine. On the EMLA phase diagram, the lidocaine curve at 10% prilocaine is at about 68 − 50 × (10/50) = 58 °C (straight‑line reading), so this mixture is not fully melted until about 58 °C: at 25 °C, crystals of lidocaine + liquid remain. Only the 1:1 ratio reaches the lowest melting point (18 °C).

The wrong recipe does not reach the low melting point.

7. Solid Dispersions: Types, Mechanism & Benefits

7 questions

Question 34 of 45
A solid dispersion is a pharmaceutical technique in which:
A. A drug is converted into a gas for inhalation
B. A highly water‑soluble drug is coated with wax to slow its release
C. A drug is dissolved in oil to make an injection
D. A poorly water‑soluble drug is mixed with a carrier substance

D. A poorly water‑soluble drug is mixed with a carrier substance

A Solid Dispersion → a pharmaceutical technique where a poorly water‑soluble drug is mixed with a carrier substance (often a polymer or something that dissolves easily in water, like urea or polyethylene glycol (PEG)).

Hide a poorly soluble drug inside an easily soluble carrier.

Question 35 of 45
In the picture below (shown in the lecture), which structure represents an amorphous solid?
A. The disordered, randomly connected network
B. The regular, repeating hexagonal lattice
C. A structure in which every atom sits in a fixed, repeating position
D. A single large, perfectly ordered crystal

A. The disordered, randomly connected network

The lecture’s picture compares crystalline solids (regular repeating lattice, left) with amorphous solids (disordered network, right). Amorphous dispersions contain the drug in this disordered, non‑crystalline form.

Crystalline = ordered pattern; amorphous = no pattern.

Question 36 of 45
When the solid dispersion is put into water (or reaches the GI tract), what happens first?
A. Both form one large crystal
B. The drug dissolves first and leaves the carrier behind as large crystals
C. The drug evaporates
D. The carrier dissolves quickly and leaves drug molecules already in a finely divided state

D. The carrier dissolves quickly and leaves drug molecules already in a finely divided state

Flowchart: drug molecules turn into tiny microcrystals → when we put this solid dispersion into water (or it reaches the GI tract) → carrier dissolves quickly → leaves drug molecules already in a finely divided state.

The wrapper dissolves away, leaving the drug as fine dust.

Question 37 of 45
In the plasma‑concentration graph below, what are the approximate peak plasma concentrations for the solid dispersion (SD) and for the normal crystalline drug?
A. SD ≈ 2100 ng/mL; normal ≈ 200 ng/mL
B. SD ≈ 500 ng/mL; normal ≈ 1500 ng/mL
C. Both ≈ 1000 ng/mL
D. SD ≈ 200 ng/mL; normal ≈ 2100 ng/mL

A. SD ≈ 2100 ng/mL; normal ≈ 200 ng/mL

The red SD curve peaks at about 2100 ng/mL (around 4 h); the blue “Normal” (crystalline drug) curve peaks at only about 200 ng/mL (around 1 h). (Note: the graph legend misspells “Normal” as “Nomal” and the axis title writes “administation” for “administration”.)

The solid dispersion puts far more drug into the blood.

Question 38 of 45
Match the type of solid dispersion: “the drug is completely dissolved in the carrier as a single‑phase solid.”
A. Eutectic mixture
B. Solid solution
C. Simple suspension
D. Amorphous dispersion

B. Solid solution

Solid solution: drug is completely dissolved in the carrier as a single‑phase solid. Eutectic mixture: drug + carrier crystallise separately, in a very fine (microcrystalline) form. Amorphous dispersion: drug in a disordered, non‑crystalline form.

Like sugar dissolved in water, but everything is solid.

Question 39 of 45
Besides the surface area effect, what second reason is given for the faster dissolution of solid dispersions?
A. The carrier increases the melting point of the drug
B. The carrier makes the drug crystals larger
C. Carrier effect: hydrophilic carriers increase wettability, so water reaches the drug surface more easily
D. The carrier reacts with the drug to form a salt

C. Carrier effect: hydrophilic carriers increase wettability, so water reaches the drug surface more easily

Why microcrystalline or amorphous forms dissolve faster: (1) surface area effect — smaller particles → more surface area; (2) carrier effect — hydrophilic carriers increase wettability → water can reach the drug surface more easily.

A water‑loving carrier pulls water onto the drug.

Question 40 of 45
A 250 mg tablet is taken and 75 mg of the drug reaches the bloodstream. What is its bioavailability?
A. 33%
B. 3.3%
C. 75%
D. 30%

D. 30%

Bioavailability = 75 mg ÷ 250 mg × 100 = 30%.

Divide what reached the blood by the dose.

8. Eutectic Mixtures in Daily Life

3 questions

Question 41 of 45
Why does ice cream stay soft in the freezer?
A. The freezer is warmer than 0 °C
B. The fat in ice cream raises the freezing point
C. The sugar + water mixture has a lower freezing point
D. Ice cream contains no water

C. The sugar + water mixture has a lower freezing point

Ice cream: sugar + water mixture → lower freezing point → keeps ice cream soft in the freezer (instead of becoming rock‑hard ice). (The study guide calls this “eutectic‑like behaviour”: strictly, sugar lowers the freezing point of water.)

Sugar stops the water from freezing completely hard.

Question 42 of 45
With typical road salt (~10% salt) the melting point of ice falls to about −6 °C. On which day would this salt level fully melt the ice on a road?
A. A day at −10 °C
B. A day at −25 °C
C. A day at −3 °C
D. A day at −15 °C

C. A day at −3 °C

With ~10% salt the melting point is ≈ −6 °C, so the ice fully melts only when the road is warmer than −6 °C: −3 °C. At −10 °C and −15 °C (colder than −6 °C) this salt level is not enough to melt all the ice; more salt, closer to the eutectic composition (~23%), is needed. At −25 °C, below the eutectic temperature (≈ −21 °C), no amount of salt can melt the ice.

The road must be warmer than the new melting point for the ice to melt completely.

Question 43 of 45
A saltwater mixture at the eutectic composition is cooled to −25 °C. What happens?
A. It boils
B. It stays liquid at every temperature
C. It melts further
D. It freezes, because −25 °C is below the lowest possible melting point (about −21 °C)

D. It freezes, because −25 °C is below the lowest possible melting point (about −21 °C)

At the eutectic composition (~23.3% NaCl) the lowest melting point is about −21.1 °C → below this temperature, the saltwater mixture will freeze. −25 °C < −21.1 °C.

Even the best salt mixture has a limit: about −21 °C.

9. Menthol–Camphor Eutectic System

2 questions

Question 44 of 45
According to the poster, what are the eutectic ratio and the eutectic temperature of the menthol–camphor system?
A. About 65:35 by weight; about −21 °C
B. About 1:1 by weight; about 19–22 °C, so it is liquid at room temperature
C. About 1:1 by weight; about 110 °C
D. About 1:2 molar; about 12 °C

B. About 1:1 by weight; about 19–22 °C, so it is liquid at room temperature

Eutectic ratio: about 1:1 (by weight) forms the eutectic composition. Eutectic temperature: the lowest melting point is around 19–22 °C; it is liquid at room temperature. The poster's diagram marks E at ≈50% menthol, 21 °C.

Half menthol, half camphor melts near room temperature.

Question 45 of 45
Which is NOT one of the pharmaceutical applications of the menthol–camphor eutectic listed on the poster?
A. Transdermal drug delivery (enhancing drug penetration through the skin)
B. Enhanced solubility, permeability and bioavailability of poorly soluble drugs
C. Raising the melting point of tablets so they resist heat
D. Pain‑relief creams, gels and lotions for muscle and joint pain

C. Raising the melting point of tablets so they resist heat

Pharmaceutical applications on the poster: enhanced solubility, permeability and bioavailability (improves dissolution and absorption of poorly soluble drugs); transdermal drug delivery (enhancing drug penetration through the skin); pain‑relief formulations (creams, gels, lotions for muscle and joint pain); potential for other drug forms (Newtonian flow → suitable as a carrier or solvent). Raising melting points is the opposite of what a eutectic does.

Eutectics lower melting points; they do not raise them.

1. Ternary Systems & the Triangle Diagram

6 questions

Question 1 of 50
A ternary system is a system that consists of:
A. One component in three states
B. Two components only
C. Three different components (A, B, C)
D. Three phases of one substance

C. Three different components (A, B, C)

Lecture: “A Ternary system is a system consisting of three different components (A, B, C).”

“Ternary” = three components.

Question 2 of 50
Why do pharmacists need ternary systems?
A. Because two-component mixtures cannot be made
B. In real life formulations usually have three or more components
C. Because ternary systems are only used for gases
D. Because most medicines contain only one ingredient

B. In real life formulations usually have three or more components

Lecture: “In real life, we rarely work with just one or two ingredients, formulations usually have three or more components.”

Real formulations are rarely just one or two ingredients.

Question 3 of 50
Which example of a three-component formulation is given in the lecture?
A. Drug + Air + Light
B. Drug + Solvent + Water
C. Water + Ice + Steam
D. Drug + Tablet + Capsule

B. Drug + Solvent + Water

Lecture: “Example: Drug + Solvent + Water”

Three different ingredients mixed together.

Question 4 of 50
A ternary diagram is:
A. A straight-line graph of two components
B. A bar chart of three separate drugs
C. A triangular graph that represents the composition of mixtures of three components adding up to 100%
D. A circular graph of temperature against pressure

C. A triangular graph that represents the composition of mixtures of three components adding up to 100%

Lecture: “A Ternary diagram is a triangular graph used to represent the composition of mixtures with three components that add up to 100%.”

A triangle, three components, total 100%.

Question 5 of 50
The blue point (near corner A) in the lecture diagram has the composition:
A. A = 10%, B = 20%, C = 70%
B. A = 5%, B = 75%, C = 20%
C. A = 20%, B = 50%, C = 30%
D. A = 70%, B = 20%, C = 10%

D. A = 70%, B = 20%, C = 10%

Lecture: “Blue point: A=70%, B=20%, C=10% (total 100)”

Close to corner A → mostly A.

Question 6 of 50
A ternary diagram is drawn at:
A. Constant volume only
B. Changing temperature and pressure
C. Constant temperature and pressure
D. The boiling point of water

C. Constant temperature and pressure

Lecture: “The triangle is a map of all possible mixtures of three components at constant T & P.”

T and P are fixed; only composition changes.

2. Reading the Triangle: Corners, Sides & Inside

4 questions

Question 7 of 50
The top corner of the triangle represents:
A. 100% A, 0% B, 0% C
B. 100% B, 0% A, 0% C
C. 100% C, 0% A, 0% B
D. 50% A, 50% C

B. 100% B, 0% A, 0% C

Lecture: “Top corner: 100% B, 0% A, 0% C”

B sits at the top.

Question 8 of 50
Line BC represents a mixture of:
A. B and C only (0% A)
B. A and B only (0% C)
C. Pure B
D. A and C only (0% B)

A. B and C only (0% A)

Lecture: “Line BC = mixture of B & C (0% A)”

The missing letter is the 0% component.

Question 9 of 50
Each side of the triangle is divided into:
A. 100 equal parts, so you can read percentages
B. 1000 parts, one per mL
C. 3 equal parts
D. 10 unequal parts

A. 100 equal parts, so you can read percentages

Lecture: “Each side is divided into 100 equal parts so you can read percentages.”

100 parts = 100%.

Question 10 of 50
Which statement about the triangle is correct?
A. Corners = 2-component mixtures, edges = pure components, inside = empty
B. Corners = pure components, edges = 2-component mixtures, inside = 3-component mixtures
C. All points represent pure components
D. Corners = 3-component mixtures, edges = pure components, inside = gases

B. Corners = pure components, edges = 2-component mixtures, inside = 3-component mixtures

Lecture: “Corners (A, B, C): 100% of one component, 0% of others. Edges (AB, BC, AC): 2-component mixtures. Inside points: Mixture of all 3 components.”

Corner → side → inside = 1 → 2 → 3 components.

3. Points on the Sides (Binary Mixtures)

5 questions

A. The BC side
B. The AB side
C. The AC side
D. Inside the triangle

B. The AB side

Lecture: “Point y = 50% B + 50% A (0% C) → lies on AB side (2 components).”

C = 0% → it is on side AB.

B = 0% puts it on the base AC.

4. Reading a Point Inside: Step by Step

6 questions

Question 16 of 50
The general rule for reading a ternary diagram is:
A. Draw all lines parallel to the base
B. Draw each line perpendicular to the nearest side
C. Draw each line parallel to the side opposite the component you are measuring
D. Draw each line towards the corner of the component

C. Draw each line parallel to the side opposite the component you are measuring

Lecture: “Rule: draw each line parallel to the side opposite the component you are measuring.”

Opposite side → parallel line.

Question 17 of 50
What is the composition of point P?
A. A = 25%, B = 35%, C = 40%
B. A = 40%, B = 35%, C = 25%
C. A = 25%, B = 40%, C = 35%
D. A = 35%, B = 25%, C = 40%

A. A = 25%, B = 35%, C = 40%

Lecture: “A = 25% · B = 35% · C = 40% … 25% + 35% + 40% = 100%”

Three parallel lines, three readings, total 100.

Question 18 of 50
You read a point and get A = 30%, B = 40%, C = 40%. What does this mean?
A. The point is outside the triangle and still correct
B. One line was drawn or read incorrectly, because the total is not 100%
C. The answer is correct because each value is below 100%
D. The mixture is supersaturated

B. One line was drawn or read incorrectly, because the total is not 100%

Lecture: “If the total is not 100%, one line was drawn or read incorrectly.”

30 + 40 + 40 = 110 ≠ 100 → re-check.

Question 19 of 50
What is the composition of point Q?
A. A = 25%, B = 60%, C = 15%
B. A = 15%, B = 60%, C = 25%
C. A = 15%, B = 25%, C = 60%
D. A = 60%, B = 15%, C = 25%

B. A = 15%, B = 60%, C = 25%

Lecture: “Step 1: line ∥ BC A = 15% · Step 2: line ∥ AC B = 60% · Step 3: line ∥ AB C = 25% · 15% + 60% + 25% = 100%”

Q is near B, so B is the biggest.

Question 20 of 50
What is the composition of point R?
A. A = 45%, B = 20%, C = 35%
B. A = 20%, B = 45%, C = 35%
C. A = 35%, B = 20%, C = 45%
D. A = 45%, B = 35%, C = 20%

A. A = 45%, B = 20%, C = 35%

Lecture: “Step 1: line ∥ BC A = 45% · Step 2: line ∥ AC B = 20% · Step 3: line ∥ AB C = 35% · 45% + 20% + 35% = 100%”

R is low and left → A biggest, B smallest.

Question 21 of 50
A point lies very close to the bottom side AC. What can you say about it?
A. It contains no C
B. It contains very little B
C. It contains very little A
D. It is almost pure B

B. It contains very little B

Lecture: “Bottom left: 100% A … Line AC = mixture of A & C (0% B)”

The base AC is where B = 0%, so near it B is small.

No answer on the slide — solved by drawing the three lines: A ≈ 17% (∥ BC), B ≈ 50% (∥ AC, E is at the half-height line), C ≈ 33% (∥ AB); total 100%.

E sits on the 50% B line, nearer side BC.

In the benzene–ethanol–water diagram, the black point is measured as:

2 + 42 + 56 = 100.

Question 25 of 50

Biggest number = water.
