8. Constant Ratio & Parallel Lines

5 questions

Question 33 of 50
Line DC in the lecture diagram (from corner C) keeps which ratio constant?
A. A : B
B. A : C
C. B : C
D. C : water

A. A : B

Lecture: “Example: Line DC = constant A:B ratio.”

A line from corner C changes C but keeps A : B fixed.

Question 34 of 50
Point D on line DC has A : B = 25 : 75. This ratio equals:
A. 3 : 1
B. 1 : 2
C. 1 : 1
D. 1 : 3

D. 1 : 3

Lecture: “D → A:B=25:75=1:3”

25 ÷ 25 = 1, 75 ÷ 25 = 3.

Question 35 of 50
As you move along line DC towards corner C, what happens?
A. The % of C increases while the A : B ratio stays 1 : 3
B. The A : B ratio changes to 3 : 1
C. The mixture becomes pure A
D. The % of C decreases

A. The % of C increases while the A : B ratio stays 1 : 3

Lecture: “D → A:B=25:75=1:3 · E → A:B=17:50=1:3 · F → A:B=4:12=1:3”

Moving to C adds C; A and B shrink together.

Question 36 of 50
Line HI is parallel to AB. All points on HI have:
A. The same A : B ratio
B. 0% C
C. The same % of C (20%), but A and B vary
D. The same % of A

C. The same % of C (20%), but A and B vary

Lecture: “Line HI is parallel to AB → all points have the same % of C (20%), but A and B vary.”

Parallel to AB = constant C.

Question 37 of 50
Which pair is correct?
A. Both lines show constant temperature
B. Both lines show pure components
C. Apex → opposite side = constant ratio of 2 components; parallel line = constant % of one component
D. Apex line = constant % of one component; parallel line = constant ratio

C. Apex → opposite side = constant ratio of 2 components; parallel line = constant % of one component

Lecture: “Apex → opposite side = constant ratio of 2 components. Parallel lines = constant % of one component (e.g., Line HI → C = 20%).”

Corner line = ratio; parallel line = one %.

9. Phase Boundaries: One Phase vs Two Phases

7 questions

Question 38 of 50
In the benzene–ethanol–water system, ethanol acts as a:
A. Solid diluent
B. Cosolvent (bridging agent) that helps benzene and water mix
C. Precipitating agent
D. Emulsion breaker

B. Cosolvent (bridging agent) that helps benzene and water mix

Lecture: “…but the presence of ethanol acts as a cosolvent (bridging agent), helping them mix and forming a single homogeneous phase until the solubility limit is reached.”

Ethanol mixes with both, so it bridges them.

Question 39 of 50
With enough ethanol, benzene and water form a single homogeneous phase until:
A. The benzene freezes
B. The temperature reaches 100 °C
C. All the water evaporates
D. The solubility limit is reached

D. The solubility limit is reached

Lecture: “…forming a single homogeneous phase until the solubility limit is reached.”

Past the limit, it separates into two phases.

Question 40 of 50
In the benzene–ethanol–water diagram, the region above the curve is:
A. 2 phases (turbidity)
B. 1 phase (clear)
C. Solid only
D. Gas only

B. 1 phase (clear)

Lecture diagram labels the area above the curve “1phase (clear)” and below it “2 phases (turbidity)”.

Lots of ethanol (top) → clear single phase.

Question 41 of 50
The numbered points 1–8 on the benzene–ethanol–water curve represent:
A. Eight different temperatures
B. Points that are all in the two-phase region
C. The compositions at the boundary between one phase and two phases
D. Eight pure components

C. The compositions at the boundary between one phase and two phases

The curve through points 1–8 separates “1phase (clear)” above from “2 phases (turbidity)” below — each flask gives one point on this boundary.

Each flask gives one point on the boundary curve.

Question 42 of 50
To prepare a homogeneous mixture from the pseudoternary phase diagram, we choose a composition that lies in:
A. Exactly on a corner
B. The two-phase area
C. Outside the triangle
D. The one-phase homogeneous miscible area

D. The one-phase homogeneous miscible area

Lecture: “So when we want to prepare this as homogenous mixture we choose a composition that lies in the one phase homogenous miscible area”

Homogeneous = one phase.

Question 43 of 50
In the lecture’s pseudoternary diagram, the shaded (green) area near the water side represents:
A. A solid region
B. Pure oleic acid
C. 1 phase
D. 2 phases (phase separation)

D. 2 phases (phase separation)

Lecture figure labels: shaded area “2 phases (phase separation)”; white area “1 phase”.

Shaded = separation here.

Question 44 of 50
In the last lecture diagram (curve near corner A), the area on the A side of the curve is:
A. 2 phase
B. Pure C
C. The eutectic point
D. 1 phase

A. 2 phase

Lecture diagram: the curve divides the triangle into “2 phase” (towards A) and “1 phase” (towards B and C).

Read the labels on each side of the curve.

10. Calculating % v/v of Each Component

4 questions

Question 45 of 50
The formula used to calculate % v/v of each component is:
A. % v/v = sum of all solvents ÷ mL of one solvent
B. % v/v = mL of solvent ÷ 100
C. % v/v = mL of solvent × total volume
D. % v/v = (mL of each solvent × 100) ÷ sum of all solvents (mL)

D. % v/v = (mL of each solvent × 100) ÷ sum of all solvents (mL)

Lecture: “% v/v = ml of each solvent x 100 / Sum of all solvents (ml)”

Part ÷ whole × 100.

Question 46 of 50
What is the % v/v of benzene in flask #1 (0.5 mL in a total of 23 mL)?
A. ≈ 2.2%
B. ≈ 50%
C. ≈ 0.5%
D. ≈ 21.7%

A. ≈ 2.2%

Lecture: “% v/v = 0.5 x 100 / 23 = 2.17 %”

0.5 ÷ 23 × 100 = 2.17%.

Question 47 of 50
What is the % v/v of water in flask #1 (13 mL in 23 mL)?
A. ≈ 13%
B. ≈ 41.3%
C. ≈ 56.5%
D. ≈ 76.5%

C. ≈ 56.5%

13 × 100 ÷ 23 = 56.5%. (The slide’s table writes 56.4.)

13 ÷ 23 × 100.

Question 48 of 50
A flask contains 5 mL benzene, 5 mL ethanol and 10 mL water. What is the % v/v of each?
A. Benzene 25%, Ethanol 25%, Water 50%
B. Benzene 5%, Ethanol 5%, Water 10%
C. Benzene 50%, Ethanol 25%, Water 25%
D. Benzene 33.3%, Ethanol 33.3%, Water 33.3%

A. Benzene 25%, Ethanol 25%, Water 50%

Lecture: “% v/v = ml of each solvent x 100 / Sum of all solvents (ml)” → total = 20 mL; 5/20 × 100 = 25%, 10/20 × 100 = 50%.

Divide each by the total (20 mL).

11. Summary & Applications

2 questions

Question 49 of 50
Which statement from the summary is correct?
A. Two different points can have the same composition
B. Each point = one temperature
C. Points inside contain only two components
D. Each point in the triangle = one unique composition

D. Each point in the triangle = one unique composition

Lecture: “Each point = one unique composition.”

One point, one mixture.

Question 50 of 50
Ternary diagrams are widely used to study:
A. Drug half-life
B. Only the boiling point of water
C. Mixtures, solubility and phase behaviour
D. Tablet hardness

C. Mixtures, solubility and phase behaviour

Lecture: “This ternary diagram is widely used in chemistry, pharmacy, and material science to study mixtures, solubility, or phase behavior.”

Mixtures and how many phases they form.

Lecture 14Surface & Interfacial Tension

40 questions

1. Interesting Phenomena2. Surface & Interface3. Origin of Surface Tension: Cohesive & Adhesive Forces4. Definition & Units of Surface Tension5. Surface Free Energy & Spherical Drops6. Surface Tension of Different Liquids7. Effect of Temperature on Surface Tension8. Interfacial Tension9. Interfacial Tension Against Water & Miscibility

1. Interesting Phenomena

2 questions

Question 1 of 40
A paper clip can rest on the surface of water without sinking. This is mainly due to:
A. Surface tension
B. Viscosity
C. Osmotic pressure
D. Density of the clip

A. Surface tension

Lecture: “Why is the coin/paper clip retained on water surface?” — the surface behaves like a stretched membrane because of surface tension.

The surface acts like a stretched skin.

Question 2 of 40
Water “beading” on a leaf into small round drops is an example of:
A. Evaporation
B. Adsorption of water into the leaf
C. Sublimation
D. Droplets taking a spherical shape due to surface tension

D. Droplets taking a spherical shape due to surface tension

Slide: “Water beading on a leaf — Why are water droplets spherical?” A sphere has the smallest surface area per unit volume.

Drops minimise their surface → spheres.

2. Surface & Interface

5 questions

Question 3 of 40
A “surface” is defined as:
A. The boundary between 2 immiscible liquids
B. The inside (bulk) of a liquid
C. The boundary between 2 phases, one of them in the gaseous state
D. The boundary between 2 solids

C. The boundary between 2 phases, one of them in the gaseous state

Lecture: “Surface : boundary between 2 phases, one of them is in the gaseous state”

Surface = something meets a gas (air).

Question 4 of 40
An “interface” is defined as:
A. A mixture of two miscible liquids
B. The centre of a liquid
C. The boundary between 2 immiscible phases
D. The boundary between a liquid and a gas only

C. The boundary between 2 immiscible phases

Lecture: “Interface: boundary between 2 immiscible phases”

Interface = two phases that don’t mix.

Question 5 of 40
The tension at a liquid/liquid boundary is called:
A. Adsorption
B. Surface tension
C. Interfacial tension
D. Wetting

C. Interfacial tension

Lecture diagram: the liquid–liquid boundary is labelled “INTERFACE” → INTERFACIAL TENSION.

Two liquids → interfacial tension.

Question 6 of 40
In the lecture’s surface/interface diagram, the gas–solid boundary is linked to:
A. Adsorption
B. Surface tension of liquids
C. Wetting
D. Interfacial tension

A. Adsorption

Lecture diagram: the gas/solid surface is labelled ADSORPTION (molecules sticking on an adsorbent).

Gas molecules collect on a solid surface.

Question 7 of 40
In the lecture’s diagram, the solid–liquid interface is linked to:
A. Evaporation
B. Wetting
C. Adsorption
D. Surface tension

B. Wetting

Lecture diagram: the liquid/solid interface is labelled WETTING (e.g. a tablet in water).

A liquid spreading on / entering a solid = wetting.

3. Origin of Surface Tension: Cohesive & Adhesive Forces

5 questions

Question 8 of 40
Molecules in the bulk of a liquid are:
A. Attracted only by air molecules
B. Surrounded in all directions by similar molecules with equal attraction
C. Not attracted at all
D. Attracted only downwards

B. Surrounded in all directions by similar molecules with equal attraction

Lecture: “Molecules in the bulk of the liquid are surrounded in all directions by similar neighboring molecules for which they have an equal attraction”

In the bulk, the pulls cancel out.

Question 9 of 40
Cohesive forces are:
A. Repulsive forces between ions
B. Attractive forces between unlike molecules
C. Forces between a liquid and its container only
D. Intermolecular attractive forces between like molecules

D. Intermolecular attractive forces between like molecules

Lecture: “Cohesive forces are well developed (intermolecular attractive forces between like molecules).”

Cohesive = same with same.

Question 10 of 40
Adhesive forces are:
A. Attractive forces between like molecules
B. Repulsive forces between water molecules
C. Gravitational forces
D. Attractive forces between unlike molecules

D. Attractive forces between unlike molecules

Lecture: “…weak adhesive forces (attractive forces between unlike molecules)”

Adhesive = different molecules.

Question 11 of 40
Besides cohesive forces, surface molecules also have weak adhesive forces with:
A. The bulk molecules only
B. Only other water molecules
C. Nothing
D. Air molecules on the top and the container on the sides

D. Air molecules on the top and the container on the sides

Lecture: “In addition, weak adhesive forces … with air molecules on the top and with the container on the sides.”

Adhesion to air (top) and walls (sides).

Question 12 of 40
The net force on a molecule at the surface is directed:
A. Up into the air
B. Into the bulk of the liquid
C. Parallel to the surface only
D. Zero, like in the bulk

B. Into the bulk of the liquid

Lecture figure: “Net force on molecule at surface is into bulk of the liquid”, while in the bulk ΣF = 0.

Unbalanced pull → inward.

4. Definition & Units of Surface Tension

5 questions

Question 13 of 40
Surface tension can be defined as:
A. The energy needed to boil a liquid
B. The force per unit length that must be applied to counterbalance the net inward pull
C. The force per unit area pushing outward
D. The mass of liquid per unit volume

B. The force per unit length that must be applied to counterbalance the net inward pull

Lecture: “Surface tension can be defined as the force per unit length that must be applied to counterbalance the net inward pull.”

Force ÷ length.

Question 14 of 40
Which is NOT a unit of surface tension as force per unit length?
A. mN m⁻¹
B. dyne/cm
C. g/cm³
D. N m⁻¹

C. g/cm³

Lecture: “( Units: dyne/cm, Nm-1, mNm-1)”

g/cm³ is density.

Question 15 of 40
Surface tension can also be defined as:
A. The work done to increase the surface area of a mass of liquid by 1 cm²
B. The heat needed to raise the temperature by 1 °C
C. The force needed to compress the liquid
D. The work needed to evaporate 1 g of liquid

A. The work done to increase the surface area of a mass of liquid by 1 cm²

Lecture: “It can also be defined as the work done to increase the surface area of a mass of liquid by 1 cm². (Units: erg/cm²)”

Work ÷ area.

Question 16 of 40
When surface tension is defined as work per unit area, its unit is:
A. J/mol
B. g/mL
C. dyne/cm²
D. erg/cm²

D. erg/cm²

Lecture: “…work done to increase the surface area … by 1 cm². (Units: erg/cm²)”

Work (erg) per area (cm²).

Question 17 of 40
The surface tension of water is 73 dyne/cm at 25 °C. This means that:
A. Water weighs 73 g per cm
B. 73 dyne is needed to boil 1 cm³ of water
C. A force of 73 dyne must be applied parallel to the water surface to increase the surface by 1 cm
D. 73 dyne must be applied perpendicular to the surface

C. A force of 73 dyne must be applied parallel to the water surface to increase the surface by 1 cm

Lecture: “A force of 73 dyne must be applied parallel to water surface to increase the surface of water by 1 cm.”

Force per unit length, parallel to the surface.

5. Surface Free Energy & Spherical Drops

4 questions

Question 18 of 40
The excess energy of surface molecules is called:
A. Activation energy
B. Kinetic energy
C. Lattice energy
D. Surface free energy

D. Surface free energy

Lecture: “This energy is called surface free energy.”

Excess energy at the surface = surface free energy.

Question 19 of 40
The surface free energy is proportional to:
A. The temperature only
B. The colour of the liquid
C. The volume of the container
D. The size of the surface

D. The size of the surface

Lecture: “The surface free energy is proportional to the size of the surface.”

Bigger surface → more energy.

Question 20 of 40
Liquid droplets tend to assume a spherical shape because:
A. A sphere has the largest surface area
B. A sphere has the smallest surface area per unit volume
C. Air pressure is highest on spheres
D. Gravity pulls drops into spheres

B. A sphere has the smallest surface area per unit volume

Lecture: “Liquid droplets tend to assume a spherical shape because a sphere has the smallest surface area per unit volume.”

Smallest area → lowest surface energy.

Question 21 of 40
When water is broken into a fine spray:
A. Its surface decreases
B. Its energy decreases
C. Its surface tension becomes zero
D. Its surface increases and the energy of the liquid also increases

D. Its surface increases and the energy of the liquid also increases

Lecture: “If the surface of the liquid increases (e.g., when water is broken into a fine spray), the energy of the liquid also increases.”

Many tiny drops = much more surface.

6. Surface Tension of Different Liquids

4 questions

Question 22 of 40
According to the lecture table, which liquid has the highest surface tension?
A. Water (72.8 dynes/cm)
B. Chloroform (27.1)
C. Glycerin (63.4)
D. Castor oil (39.0)

A. Water (72.8 dynes/cm)

Lecture table: Water 72.8, Glycerin 63.4, Oleic acid 32.5, Benzene 28.9, Chloroform 27.1, Carbon tetrachloride 26.7, Castor oil 39.0, Olive oil 35.8, Cottonseed oil 35.4, Liquid petrolatum 33.1.

Water is the top of the list.

Question 23 of 40
According to the table, which liquid has the lowest surface tension?
A. Liquid petrolatum (33.1)
B. Chloroform (27.1)
C. Carbon tetrachloride (26.7)
D. Benzene (28.9)

C. Carbon tetrachloride (26.7)

Lecture table: Carbon tetrachloride 26.7 dynes/cm — the lowest value listed.

Smallest number in the table.

Question 24 of 40
Complete the lecture statement: “The higher the surface tension, the ______ the intermolecular forces.”
A. Weaker
B. Same
C. Stronger
D. Zero

C. Stronger

Slide question: “The higher the surface tension, the …… (stronger/weaker) the intermolecular forces.” Stronger cohesive forces → stronger inward pull → higher γ.

Surface tension comes from cohesive forces.

Question 25 of 40
Complete the lecture statement: “Volatile liquids have ______ surface tension.”
A. Low
B. High
C. The same as water
D. Infinite

A. Low

Slide question: “Volatile liquids have ….. (high/low) surface tension.” Volatile liquids have weak intermolecular forces, so they evaporate easily and have low γ.

Weak forces → easy evaporation and low γ.

7. Effect of Temperature on Surface Tension

5 questions

Question 26 of 40
The relation between surface tension and temperature is:
A. No relation
B. Direct — it increases with temperature
C. It first increases then decreases
D. Inverse — surface tension decreases as temperature increases

D. Inverse — surface tension decreases as temperature increases

Lecture: “There is an inverse relation between surface tension & temperature”

γ ∝ 1/temperature.

Question 27 of 40
Why does increasing temperature decrease surface tension?
A. Heat removes the surface
B. Heat makes the cohesive forces stronger
C. Heat increases the inward pull
D. More thermal movement (kinetic energy) weakens cohesive forces, so less force is needed to balance the inward pull

D. More thermal movement (kinetic energy) weakens cohesive forces, so less force is needed to balance the inward pull

Lecture: “Increase in temperature → increase in the thermal movement of molecules (kinetic energy) → weaker cohesive forces → the force needed to counterbalance the inward force is decreased.”

Hot molecules move more and hold each other less.

Question 28 of 40
Why must surface tension be defined at a specific temperature?
A. Because temperature has no effect
B. Because units change with temperature
C. Because surface tension changes with temperature
D. Because it only exists at 25 °C

C. Because surface tension changes with temperature

Lecture: “Surface tension should be defined at specific temperature.”

Different T → different γ.

Question 29 of 40
By how much does the surface tension of water fall between 20 °C and 100 °C (Table 3.1)?
A. 14.8 dynes/cm
B. 72.8 dynes/cm
C. 18.5 dynes/cm
D. 5.3 dynes/cm

A. 14.8 dynes/cm

Table 3.1: 72.8 − 58.0 = 14.8 dynes/cm.

Subtract the two values.

Question 30 of 40
Hot water is a better cleaning agent than cold water because:
A. Hot water has lower surface tension, so it gets better into pores and fissures
B. Cold water penetrates pores better
C. Hot water has higher surface tension
D. Hot water forms bridges between pores

A. Hot water has lower surface tension, so it gets better into pores and fissures

Lecture: “Hot water has lower surface tension, so can get better into pores and fissures, while cold water with high surface tension can form only bridges between them.”

Lower γ → better penetration.

8. Interfacial Tension

5 questions

Question 31 of 40
Interfacial tension is caused by:
A. Chemical reaction between the liquids
B. Gravity acting on the lower phase
C. The imbalance of intermolecular forces at the interface
D. Evaporation of one liquid

C. The imbalance of intermolecular forces at the interface

Lecture: “The interfacial tension is caused by the imbalance of intermolecular forces at the interface, where molecules are pulled more strongly by the neighboring phase than by the phase on the other side.”

Same idea as surface tension, but liquid vs liquid.

Question 32 of 40
The force of interfacial tension causes the interface to behave like:
A. A solid wall
B. A stretched membrane
C. A sponge
D. A gas layer

B. A stretched membrane

Lecture: “This force causes the interface to behave like a stretched membrane.”

Same “stretched skin” idea.

Question 33 of 40
Interfacial tension can be defined as:
A. The force per unit length existing at the interface between two immiscible liquid phases
B. The weight of the upper liquid
C. The force per unit area at a gas–liquid surface
D. The pressure inside a bubble

A. The force per unit length existing at the interface between two immiscible liquid phases

Lecture: “It can be defined as the force per unit length existing at the interface between two immiscible liquid phases.”

Force per length at a liquid/liquid boundary.

Question 34 of 40
Examples of liquid–liquid interfaces in pharmacy include:
A. Powders in air
B. Gas in a cylinder
C. O/W and W/O emulsions
D. Tablets in water

C. O/W and W/O emulsions

Lecture slide “Liquid-liquid interface”: O/W emulsion and W/O emulsion (oil and water).

Emulsions = oil + water droplets.

Question 35 of 40
Examples of solid–liquid interfaces in the lecture include:
A. Water vapour in air
B. Solid particles dispersed in a liquid (suspension) and a tablet in water
C. Oil droplets in water
D. Air bubbles in soap

B. Solid particles dispersed in a liquid (suspension) and a tablet in water

Lecture slide “Solid-liquid interface”: solid particles dispersed (disperse phase solid, continuous phase liquid) and a tablet dissolving in water.

Solid touching liquid.

9. Interfacial Tension Against Water & Miscibility

5 questions

Question 36 of 40
A high interfacial tension means:
A. Low interaction, low adhesive forces, high cohesive forces → immiscible liquids
B. The liquids are identical
C. High interaction → miscible liquids
D. High adhesive forces

A. Low interaction, low adhesive forces, high cohesive forces → immiscible liquids

Lecture: “High interfacial tension = low interaction between the 2 liquids = low adhesive forces & high cohesive forces = immiscible liquids”

High IFT = they don’t mix.

Question 37 of 40
For completely miscible liquids, the interfacial tension is:
A. Zero (no interface)
B. Equal to the surface tension of water
C. Very high
D. 72.8 dynes/cm

A. Zero (no interface)

Slide question: “For completely miscible liquids, the interfacial tension = ??” — miscible liquids form one phase, there is no interface, so interfacial tension = 0.

No boundary → no interfacial tension.

Question 38 of 40
The interfacial tension of benzene against water at 20 °C is:
A. 51.1 dynes/cm
B. 35.0 dynes/cm
C. 28.9 dynes/cm
D. 375 dynes/cm

B. 35.0 dynes/cm

Table: Benzene 35.0 (its surface tension is 28.9 — a different value).

Don’t mix up the two tables.

Question 39 of 40
Between n-hexane (51.1) and n-octyl alcohol (8.52), which interacts more with water?
A. Neither interacts at all
B. n-Octyl alcohol — lower interfacial tension means more interaction
C. n-Hexane — higher interfacial tension means more interaction
D. Both interact equally

B. n-Octyl alcohol — lower interfacial tension means more interaction

Lecture: “High interfacial tension = low interaction between the 2 liquids”

Lower IFT → more interaction (the OH group likes water).

Question 40 of 40
Which liquid in the table is the most immiscible with water (least interaction)?
A. Caprylic acid
B. Mercury
C. Ethyl ether
D. n-Octyl alcohol

B. Mercury

Lecture: “High interfacial tension = low interaction … = immiscible liquids” Mercury: 375 dynes/cm.

Highest IFT = least interaction.

Lecture 15Solid–Liquid Interface

40 questions

1. Interfaces & the Contact Angle2. Contact Angle, Cohesion & Adhesion3. Wetting, Spreading & Dewetting4. Hydrophilic & Hydrophobic Surfaces5. θ Is Specific for a Given System6. Importance of Wetting in Pharmaceutical Formulations7. Superhydrophobic (Lotus) Effect

1. Interfaces & the Contact Angle

4 questions

Question 1 of 40
If a drop of liquid is placed on a flat, smooth, horizontal solid surface, the drop will:
A. Exhibit a definite angle against the solid, known as the contact angle (θ, α)
B. Dissolve the solid
C. Always spread completely
D. Always form a perfect sphere

A. Exhibit a definite angle against the solid, known as the contact angle (θ, α)

Slide: “If a drop of liquid is placed on a flat, smooth, horizontal solid surface, the drop will exhibit a definite angle against the solid known as the contact angle (θ, α).”

Every solid–liquid pair gives its own contact angle.

Question 2 of 40
The contact angle is defined as:
A. The angle at the top of the drop
B. The angle between two liquid surfaces
C. The angle the tangent to the liquid surface makes with the solid surface over which it spreads
D. The angle between the solid surface and the vertical

C. The angle the tangent to the liquid surface makes with the solid surface over which it spreads

Slide: “Definition of the contact angle: it is the angle the tangent to the liquid surface makes with the solid surface over which it spreads.”

Draw a tangent at the edge of the drop; measure the angle inside the liquid.

Question 3 of 40
The contact angle is determined at:
A. The top of the drop
B. The point of intersection of the three interfaces: solid / liquid / gas
C. The solid–gas interface far from the drop
D. The centre of the drop

B. The point of intersection of the three interfaces: solid / liquid / gas

Slide: “θ is determined at the point of intersection of the three interfaces; solid/liquid/gas.”

The edge of the drop = where solid, liquid and air meet.

Question 4 of 40
The contact angle lies in the range of:
A. 90 – 180°
B. 0 – 90°
C. 0 – 360°
D. 0 – 180°

D. 0 – 180°

Slide: “θ lies in the range of 0 – 180°.”

0° = complete spreading; 180° = complete non-wetting.

2. Contact Angle, Cohesion & Adhesion

5 questions

Question 5 of 40
The contact angle depends on:
A. The volume of the drop only
B. The size of the container only
C. The colour of the liquid
D. The nature of the solid surface and the liquid drop

D. The nature of the solid surface and the liquid drop

Slide: “θ depends on the nature of the solid surface and the liquid drop” → θ is specific for a given system.

Change the solid or the liquid → θ changes.

Question 6 of 40
The contact angle reflects the balance between:
A. Gravity and buoyancy
B. Cohesive forces (within the solid and the liquid) and adhesive forces (between the solid and the liquid)
C. Viscosity and density
D. Temperature and pressure

B. Cohesive forces (within the solid and the liquid) and adhesive forces (between the solid and the liquid)

Slide: “θ reflects the balance between the cohesive forces within both the solid & the liquid and the adhesive forces between the solid & liquid.”

Cohesion = same molecules attract; adhesion = different molecules attract.

Question 7 of 40
A low value of θ reflects:
A. High interaction between the solid and the liquid (adhesion > cohesion)
B. Adhesion = cohesion
C. No interaction at all
D. Low interaction (adhesion < cohesion)

A. High interaction between the solid and the liquid (adhesion > cohesion)

Slide: “Low value of θ reflects high interaction between the solid surface & the liquid drop (adhesion > cohesion).”

Small angle = the drop spreads = the liquid likes the solid.

Question 8 of 40
A high value of θ reflects:
A. Low interaction between the solid and the liquid (adhesion < cohesion)
B. Adhesion = cohesion
C. Complete spreading
D. High interaction (adhesion > cohesion)

A. Low interaction between the solid and the liquid (adhesion < cohesion)

Slide: “High value of θ reflects low interaction between the solid surface & the liquid drop (adhesion < cohesion).”

Large angle = the drop stays round = the liquid prefers itself.

Question 9 of 40
θ for pure water on pure silver is 90°. This means:
A. Adhesion < cohesion
B. Complete spreading
C. Adhesion > cohesion
D. Adhesion = cohesion

D. Adhesion = cohesion

Slide: “θ for pure water–pure silver is 90° (adhesion = cohesion).”

90° is the border between wetting and non-wetting.

3. Wetting, Spreading & Dewetting

6 questions

Question 10 of 40
If the attractive forces between water and the solid surface exceed the cohesive forces in water:
A. Large contact angle (θ > 90°) → incomplete wetting
B. Small contact angle (θ < 90°) → good wetting, e.g. water on glass
C. θ = 90° always
D. θ = 180° → non-wetting

B. Small contact angle (θ < 90°) → good wetting, e.g. water on glass

Slide: “If the attractive forces between water & the solid surface exceed the cohesive forces → small contact angle (θ < 90°) → good wetting (e.g., water drop on glass surface).”

Adhesion wins → the drop spreads.

Question 11 of 40
If the attractive forces between the liquid and the solid are less than the cohesive forces in the liquid:
A. The drop dissolves the solid
B. Small contact angle (θ < 90°) → good wetting
C. θ = 0° → spreading
D. Large contact angle (θ > 90°) → incomplete wetting, e.g. water on Teflon

D. Large contact angle (θ > 90°) → incomplete wetting, e.g. water on Teflon

Slide: “… less than the cohesive forces in the liquid drop → large contact angle (θ > 90°) → incomplete wetting (e.g., water on Teflon surface).”

Cohesion wins → the drop stays rounded.

Question 12 of 40
Spreading occurs when:
A. θ = 90°
B. θ > 90°
C. θ = 180° → minimal interaction
D. θ = 0° → maximum adhesive forces

D. θ = 0° → maximum adhesive forces

Slide: “Spreading occurs when θ = zero° → maximum adhesive forces.”

The drop becomes a flat film.

Question 13 of 40
Non-wetting / dewetting takes place at:
A. θ = 180° → minimal interaction between the solid and liquid
B. θ = 45°
C. θ < 90°
D. θ = 0°

A. θ = 180° → minimal interaction between the solid and liquid

Slide: “Non-wetting/dewetting takes place at θ = 180° → minimal interaction between the solid and liquid.”

The drop is a full sphere touching one point.

Question 14 of 40
From the table: a contact angle of α < 90° means ___, while α = 90° and α > 90° mean ___.
A. Good wetting; incomplete wetting
B. Spreading; non-wetting
C. Non-wetting; spreading
D. Incomplete wetting; good wetting

A. Good wetting; incomplete wetting

Table: α = 0° → spreading · α < 90° → good wetting · α = 90° → incomplete wetting · α > 90° → incomplete wetting · α = 180° → nonwetting.

Read the table from flat drop (top) to sphere (bottom).

Question 15 of 40
By knowing the contact angle we can differentiate between:
A. Melting and boiling
B. Wetting, dewetting and spreading
C. Acids and bases
D. Solutions and suspensions only

B. Wetting, dewetting and spreading

Slide: “By knowing the contact angle (θ) we can differentiate between wetting / dewetting & spreading.”

θ tells you how well a liquid wets a solid.

4. Hydrophilic & Hydrophobic Surfaces

3 questions

Question 16 of 40
A surface with a contact angle < 90° with water is:
A. Lipophilic
B. Hydrophilic
C. Superhydrophobic
D. Hydrophobic

B. Hydrophilic

Slide: “Surfaces with a contact angle < 90° (with water) are hydrophilic.”

Hydro-philic = water-loving.

Question 17 of 40
A surface with a contact angle > 90° with water is:
A. Spreading
B. Hydrophilic
C. Hydrophobic
D. Completely wetted

C. Hydrophobic

Slide: “Surfaces with a contact angle > 90° (with water) are hydrophobic.”

Hydro-phobic = water-fearing.

Question 18 of 40
Compared with a hydrophilic surface, a hydrophobic surface has:
A. Low contact angle and poor wettability
B. High contact angle and good wettability
C. High contact angle, poor adhesiveness and poor wettability
D. Low contact angle, good adhesiveness and good wettability

C. High contact angle, poor adhesiveness and poor wettability

Figure: hydrophobic → contact angle high, adhesiveness poor, wettability poor; hydrophilic → low, good, good.

High θ ↔ poor wetting.

5. θ Is Specific for a Given System

6 questions

Question 19 of 40
θ water–copper (70.0°) < θ LiBr–copper (90.9°). This means that the adhesive forces between water and copper are stronger than those between LiBr and copper.
A. True
B. False

A. True

True — lower θ = stronger adhesion. Water on copper (70.0°) < LiBr 55% on copper (90.9°).

Smaller angle → stronger solid–liquid attraction.

Question 20 of 40
The addition of LiBr to water (LiBr solution 55%) increases its interaction with the aluminum surface.
A. True
B. False

B. False

False — on aluminum, θ increases from 63.8° (water) to 89.7° (LiBr 55%). A higher θ means less interaction.

θ went up → interaction went down.

Question 21 of 40
The stainless steel surface is better wetted with water compared to PTFE.
A. True
B. False

A. True

True — water on stainless steel θ ≈ 62.1° (< 90°, wetting) vs water on PTFE θ ≈ 106.2° (> 90°, incomplete wetting).

Lower θ = better wetting.

Question 22 of 40
Which system ensures the maximum solid–liquid interaction?
A. LiBr 55% on PTFE (θ ≈ 115.8°)
B. LiBr 55% on copper (θ ≈ 90.9°)
C. Water on stainless steel (θ ≈ 62.1°)
D. Water on PTFE (θ ≈ 106.2°)

C. Water on stainless steel (θ ≈ 62.1°)

The lowest contact angle = maximum interaction → water / stainless steel, 62.1° (aluminum 63.8°, copper 70.0°).

Look for the smallest θ in the figure.

Question 23 of 40
Which system shows the minimum solid–liquid interaction?
A. LiBr 55% on PTFE (θ ≈ 115.8°)
B. Water on aluminum (θ ≈ 63.8°)
C. Water on stainless steel (θ ≈ 62.1°)
D. Water on copper (θ ≈ 70.0°)

A. LiBr 55% on PTFE (θ ≈ 115.8°)

The highest contact angle = minimum interaction → LiBr 55% / PTFE, 115.8°.

Largest θ = poorest wetting.

Question 24 of 40
Which surface is hydrophobic in this figure?
A. PTFE (θ with water ≈ 106.2° > 90°)
B. Stainless steel
C. Copper
D. Aluminum

A. PTFE (θ with water ≈ 106.2° > 90°)

Only PTFE has a contact angle with water above 90° → hydrophobic. The metals are < 90° → hydrophilic.

θ with water > 90° → hydrophobic.

6. Importance of Wetting in Pharmaceutical Formulations

10 questions

Question 25 of 40
Why should suspended solid particles have a hydrophilic coating (wetting agent)?
A. To make the suspension hydrophobic
B. To increase particle size
C. To make the particles dissolve completely
D. To ensure good wetting and dispersion and avoid particle agglomeration

D. To ensure good wetting and dispersion and avoid particle agglomeration

Slide: “The solid particles should have hydrophilic coating on the surface (wetting agent) to ensure good wetting & dispersion & avoid particle agglomeration.”

Suspension = insoluble particles in water → they must be wetted.

Question 26 of 40
A suspension is composed of:
A. A gas dispersed in a liquid
B. Two immiscible liquids
C. Insoluble solid particles suspended in water
D. A drug completely dissolved in water

C. Insoluble solid particles suspended in water

Slide: “A suspension is composed of insoluble solid particles suspended in water.”

Key point: Insoluble solid particles suspended in water.

Question 27 of 40
What dictates how fast a tablet disintegrates?
A. The amount of air in the bottle
B. The tablet's shape only
C. The speed at which water or gastric fluid penetrates the compressed tablet matrix
D. The colour of the tablet

C. The speed at which water or gastric fluid penetrates the compressed tablet matrix

Slide: “The speed at which water or gastric fluid penetrates the compressed tablet matrix dictates how fast the tablet breaks apart (disintegrates).”

Water must get in before the tablet can break up.

Question 28 of 40
A poorly-wetted tablet leads to:
A. Poor water penetration into the tablet core → slow disintegration
B. Fast disintegration
C. Faster dissolution
D. Better swelling

A. Poor water penetration into the tablet core → slow disintegration

Slide: “Poorly-wetted tablet → poor water penetration in tablet core → slow disintegration.”

Key point: Poor water penetration into the tablet core → slow disintegration.

Question 29 of 40
Good wettability of the disintegrated particles leads to:
A. No drug release
B. Lower dissolution rate
C. Higher chance for the drug to dissolve → high dissolution / release rate
D. Agglomeration

C. Higher chance for the drug to dissolve → high dissolution / release rate

Slide: “Good wettability increases the chance for the drug to dissolve → high dissolution / release rate.”

Wetting → dissolution → release.

Question 30 of 40
To prepare immediate-release tablets, ___ excipients should be used; for sustained-release tablets, ___ excipients.
A. Hydrophobic; hydrophobic
B. Hydrophobic; hydrophilic
C. Hydrophilic; hydrophilic
D. Hydrophilic; hydrophobic

D. Hydrophilic; hydrophobic

Immediate release needs fast wetting → hydrophilic excipients. Sustained release needs slow water entry → hydrophobic excipients.

No answer on the slide — the question is asked on the slide; this follows from the wetting rule.

Question 31 of 40
In tablets that remain intact (no disintegration), the drug is released:
A. Never
B. Immediately
C. Only after the tablet melts
D. Slowly, after swelling of the intact tablet (water absorption and gel-layer formation)

D. Slowly, after swelling of the intact tablet (water absorption and gel-layer formation)

Slide: “The drug is released slowly after swelling of the intact tablet. Swelling means increase in tablet dimension following water absorption and formation of gel layer. Wetting is important to ensure good tablet swelling and adequate drug release.”

Key point: Slowly, after swelling of the intact tablet (water absorption and gel-layer formation).

Question 32 of 40
Swelling of a tablet means:
A. Breaking into fine particles
B. An increase in tablet dimension following water absorption and formation of a gel layer
C. Melting of the tablet
D. Loss of the coating

B. An increase in tablet dimension following water absorption and formation of a gel layer

Slide: “Swelling means increase in tablet dimension following water absorption and formation of gel layer.”

Key point: An increase in tablet dimension following water absorption and formation of a gel layer.

Question 33 of 40
In wet granulation, the liquid binder solution should:
A. Dissolve the granules
B. Not touch the powder
C. Have a high contact angle with the powder
D. Ensure good wetting of the powder (low θ) to form granules

D. Ensure good wetting of the powder (low θ) to form granules

Slide: “Wet granulation combines dry powders with a liquid binder to form larger, uniform agglomerates or granules. The liquid binder solution should ensure good wetting of the powder (low θ) to form granules.”

Key point: Ensure good wetting of the powder (low θ) to form granules.

Question 34 of 40
In tablet coating, the coating solution should:
A. Have θ = 180° with the tablet
B. Be able to wet the tablet surface to ensure good coating
C. Repel the tablet surface
D. Dissolve the tablet core

B. Be able to wet the tablet surface to ensure good coating

Slide: “Coating of tablets is done by applying a polymeric or sugar coating solution … The coating solution should be able to wet the surface to ensure good tablet coating.”

Key point: Be able to wet the tablet surface to ensure good coating.

7. Superhydrophobic (Lotus) Effect

6 questions

Question 35 of 40
The “lotus effect” refers to:
A. Complete spreading of water
B. The fast dissolution of tablets
C. The yellow colour of lotus flowers
D. The very high water repellence of lotus leaves (superhydrophobic effect)

D. The very high water repellence of lotus leaves (superhydrophobic effect)

Slide: “This refers to the very high water repellence exhibited by the leaves of the lotus flower.”

Key point: The very high water repellence of lotus leaves (superhydrophobic effect).

Question 36 of 40
Super-hydrophobic surfaces show contact angles up to about:
A. 90°
B. 60° — most of the drop is in contact
C. 160° — only 2–3% of a drop's surface is in contact
D. 0°

C. 160° — only 2–3% of a drop's surface is in contact

Slide: “Some plants show contact angles up to 160° and are called super-hydrophobic meaning that only 2-3% of a drop's surface is in contact.”

Key point: 160° — only 2–3% of a drop's surface is in contact.

Question 37 of 40
The superhydrophobic effect is also called:
A. The dissolution effect
B. The wetting effect
C. The self-cleaning effect
D. The spreading effect

C. The self-cleaning effect

Slide: “Superhydrophobic effect = self-cleaning effect.”

Key point: The self-cleaning effect.

Question 38 of 40
Why do superhydrophobic surfaces stay clean?
A. Dirt dissolves in the surface
B. Dirt particles have minor contact with the surface, and their adhesion to water is much higher than to the surface
C. Water spreads and soaks the dirt into the surface
D. The surface is hydrophilic

B. Dirt particles have minor contact with the surface, and their adhesion to water is much higher than to the surface

Slide: “Dirt particles, irrespective of their nature, tend to have minor contact with the surface. The adhesion between dirt particles and water is much higher than that with solid surface.” → water drops roll off and carry the dirt.

Key point: Dirt particles have minor contact with the surface, and their adhesion to water is much higher than to the surface.

Question 39 of 40
Which is a general (technical) application of the superhydrophobic effect?
A. Water-proof coatings, paints, roof tiles, fabrics and cars
B. Hydrophilic wetting agents in suspensions
C. Tablet disintegrants
D. Wet granulation binders

A. Water-proof coatings, paints, roof tiles, fabrics and cars

Slide: “Technical applications include water-proof coatings, paints, roof tiles, fabrics, cars and others → surfaces can stay dry and clean.”

Key point: Water-proof coatings, paints, roof tiles, fabrics and cars.

Question 40 of 40
Which is a pharmaceutical application of the superhydrophobic effect?
A. Oral rehydration solutions
B. Plasters, blisters & containers, water-resistant cosmetics & sunscreens, nanoparticles for drug delivery
C. Wetting agents in suspensions
D. Immediate-release tablets

B. Plasters, blisters & containers, water-resistant cosmetics & sunscreens, nanoparticles for drug delivery

Slide: applications in pharmaceutical formulation: plasters · blisters & containers · water-resistant cosmetics & sun-screens · nanoparticles for drug delivery.

Key point: Plasters, blisters & containers, water-resistant cosmetics & sunscreens, nanoparticles for drug delivery.

Lecture 16Surface Active Agents

46 questions

1. Surfactants & Surface Tension2. Micelles & CMC3. Hydrophilic–Lipophilic Balance (HLB)4. HLB & Applications of SAA5. Types of SAA Based on Their Charge6. Poloxamers7. Spans & Tweens8. Biological Surfactants

1. Surfactants & Surface Tension

6 questions

Question 1 of 46
Surface active agents (SAA, surfactants) are described as:
A. Purely hydrophilic molecules
B. Purely lipophilic molecules
C. Amphiphilic molecules
D. Inorganic salts

C. Amphiphilic molecules

Slide 2: “Surface Active agents (SAA) — Surfactants — Amphiphilic molecules”, with one part having affinity to polar, hydrophilic liquids and one part having affinity to non-polar, hydrophobic liquids.

Amphiphilic = one molecule, two personalities: a water-loving head and an oil-loving tail.

Question 2 of 46
When SAA is added to water, the molecules arrange at the surface so that:
A. The polar heads associate with water and the lipophilic tails point upwards to the air
B. All molecules dissolve evenly in the bulk
C. The tails dip into the water and the heads point to the air
D. The molecules lie flat with no orientation

A. The polar heads associate with water and the lipophilic tails point upwards to the air

Slide 3: “The polar heads of SAA will be associated with water. The lipophilic tails will be directed upwards to the air.”

Each part goes where it is happiest: head in water, tail away from water.

Question 3 of 46
Adding SAA to water affects its surface tension by:
A. Doubling it
B. Reducing it
C. Increasing it
D. Not changing it

B. Reducing it

Slide 4: “The surface tension of water is reduced; when surfactant molecules adsorb at the water surface, the surfactant molecules replace some of the water molecules in the surface.”

Surfactant at the surface = fewer water–water pulls = lower surface tension.

Question 4 of 46
Why does SAA reduce the surface tension of water?
A. Surfactants evaporate the surface water molecules
B. Surfactants raise the temperature of the water
C. Attraction between surfactant and water molecules is less than between two water molecules, so cohesive forces at the surface decrease
D. Surfactant molecules increase hydrogen bonding at the surface

C. Attraction between surfactant and water molecules is less than between two water molecules, so cohesive forces at the surface decrease

Slide 4: “The forces of attraction between surfactant and water molecules are less than those between two adjacent water molecules, hence the cohesive forces between water molecules decrease and contraction force (surface tension) is gradually reduced.”

Weaker pulls at the surface → weaker “skin” on the water.

Question 5 of 46
When oil is added to a water/SAA system, the SAA molecules orient so that:
A. All SAA stays in the water bulk
B. All SAA moves into the oil phase
C. Polar heads associate with water and lipophilic tails associate with the oil
D. Polar heads associate with the oil and tails with water

C. Polar heads associate with water and lipophilic tails associate with the oil

Slide 5: “The polar heads of SAA will be associated with water. The lipophilic tails will be associated with the oil.”

The surfactant sits at the oil–water interface, bridging the two liquids.

Question 6 of 46
SAA at an oil–water interface will:
A. Make oil and water completely immiscible
B. Reduce the interfacial tension between oil and water
C. Have no effect on interfacial tension
D. Increase the interfacial tension

B. Reduce the interfacial tension between oil and water

Slide 5 asks: “How would this affect the interfacial tension between oil & water?” By the same reasoning as slide 4, the adsorbed SAA molecules replace oil and water molecules at the interface, so the interfacial tension is reduced. (The slide only asks this; the answer is not written on the slide.)

Same idea as surface tension: surfactant at the boundary weakens the pull between the two sides.

2. Micelles & CMC

6 questions

Question 7 of 46
Micelles in water are assemblies of SAA in which:
A. Lipophilic tails face water and heads are hidden inside
B. Hydrophilic heads face water and lipophilic tails are hidden inside
C. Only tails are present
D. Heads and tails are randomly mixed

B. Hydrophilic heads face water and lipophilic tails are hidden inside

Slide 6: “Micelles are assemblies of SAA in which the hydrophilic heads are facing water and the lipophilic tails are protected (hidden) inside the aggregates.”

A micelle is a ball: heads outside touching water, tails tucked in the middle.

Question 8 of 46
The critical micelle concentration (CMC) is:
A. The concentration at which SAA starts to adsorb at the surface
B. The concentration at which surface tension equals zero
C. The maximum solubility of SAA in water
D. The concentration of SAA at which micelles start to form

D. The concentration of SAA at which micelles start to form

Slide 7: “The concentration of SAA at which micelles start to form is known as the critical micellar concentration (CMC).”

CMC = the point where micelles first appear.

Question 9 of 46
According to the graph, adding more SAA above the CMC will:
A. Increase surface tension back to ~70 mN/m
B. Increase the number of micelles without further reduction in surface tension
C. Destroy the existing micelles
D. Keep reducing surface tension sharply

B. Increase the number of micelles without further reduction in surface tension

Slide 7: “Further addition of SAA above CMC will increase the number of micelles without any further reduction in surface tension.” On the graph the line becomes flat after the CMC.

After CMC the curve is flat: extra surfactant just makes more micelles.

Question 10 of 46
On the CMC graph, the surface tension of pure water (before any SAA) is about:
A. 70 mN/m
B. 7 mN/m
C. 20 mN/m
D. 180 mN/m

A. 70 mN/m

Slide 7 graph label: “Pure Water Surface Tension ~ 70 mN/m”.

The curve starts at ~70 mN/m and falls as monomers adsorb.

Question 11 of 46
Which statement about surfactant at and above the CMC is TRUE?
A. Surface and bulk molecules never exchange
B. All surfactant molecules leave the surface
C. Micelles only form at the surface
D. There is an equilibrium between surfactant molecules at the surface and those in the bulk

D. There is an equilibrium between surfactant molecules at the surface and those in the bulk

Slide 7: “There is an equilibrium between surfactant molecules at the surface of the solution and those in the bulk of the solution.”

Molecules keep moving between the surface, free monomers and micelles.

Question 12 of 46
When SAA is added to oil or a lipophilic solution, it forms:
A. Inverted micelles: hydrophilic heads protected inside, lipophilic tails directed outwards
B. Normal micelles: heads outside, tails inside
C. Crystals
D. A monolayer only

A. Inverted micelles: hydrophilic heads protected inside, lipophilic tails directed outwards

Slide 8: “The addition of SAA to oil or lipophilic solution → “Inverted micelles” are formed: the hydrophilic heads are protected inside and the lipophilic tails are directed outwards.”

In oil everything flips: tails outside in the oil, heads hidden in the core.

3. Hydrophilic–Lipophilic Balance (HLB)

4 questions

Question 13 of 46
Whether a SAA is predominantly hydrophilic, lipophilic or balanced depends on:
A. The temperature of the room only
B. The size of the container
C. Its colour
D. The number and nature of its polar and non-polar groups

D. The number and nature of its polar and non-polar groups

Slide 9: “Depending on the number & nature of polar & non-polar groups, SAA can be predominantly hydrophilic, lipophilic or balanced in between.”

Bigger head → more water-loving; longer tail → more oil-loving.

Question 14 of 46
Ethyl alcohol, amyl alcohol and cetyl alcohol all have one OH group. Which is the most lipophilic?
A. Amyl alcohol
B. Cetyl alcohol (longest hydrocarbon chain)
C. Ethyl alcohol (shortest chain)
D. They are equally lipophilic

B. Cetyl alcohol (longest hydrocarbon chain)

Slide 9 shows the three alcohols with the same polar OH head but increasing hydrocarbon chains: ethyl (2 C) < amyl (5 C) < cetyl (16 C). With the same polar group, the longer non-polar chain makes the molecule more lipophilic.

Same head, longer tail → more oil-loving.

Question 15 of 46
HLB is:
A. An arbitrary scale (0–20) devised by Griffin to measure the hydrophilicity/lipophilicity of a SAA
B. A scale of 0–14 for acidity
C. A measure of the charge of a SAA
D. The concentration at which micelles form

A. An arbitrary scale (0–20) devised by Griffin to measure the hydrophilicity/lipophilicity of a SAA

Slide 10: “HLB is an arbitrary scale (0-20) devised by Griffin as a measure for the degree of hydrophilicity / lipophilicity of a SAA.”

HLB = how water-loving vs oil-loving a surfactant is.

Question 16 of 46
A SAA with an HLB lower than 10 is:
A. More lipophilic
B. Always a detergent
C. More hydrophilic
D. Always cationic

A. More lipophilic

Slide 10: “SAA with low HLB (< 10) are more lipophilic. SAA with high HLB (> 10) are more hydrophilic.”

Low number = oil-loving; high number = water-loving.

4. HLB & Applications of SAA

7 questions

Question 17 of 46
Extremely lipophilic SAA (HLB < 3) are commonly used as:
A. O/W emulsifiers
B. Solubilizers
C. Antifoaming agents
D. Detergents

C. Antifoaming agents

Slide 11: “Extremely lipophilic SAA (HLB < 3) are commonly used as antifoaming agents.”

Bottom of the scale = breaks foam.

Question 18 of 46
Lipophilic SAA with HLB 3–8 can be used as:
A. Emulsifiers to prepare O/W emulsions
B. Solubilizers
C. Detergents
D. Emulsifiers to prepare W/O emulsions

D. Emulsifiers to prepare W/O emulsions

Slide 11: “Lipophilic SAA (HLB 3-8) can be used as emulsifier to prepare W/O emulsion.”

Oil-loving surfactant → oil is the outside phase → W/O.

Question 19 of 46
Hydrophilic SAA with HLB 8–16 can be used as emulsifiers for:
A. Antifoaming
B. Inverted micelles only
C. W/O emulsions
D. O/W emulsions

D. O/W emulsions

Slide 11: “Hydrophilic SAA (HLB 8-16) can be used as emulsifier for O/W emulsion.”

Water-loving surfactant → water is the outside phase → O/W.

Question 20 of 46
SAA with a balanced hydrophilic/lipophilic character (HLB 7–9) tend to be used as:
A. Antifoaming agents
B. Detergents
C. Solubilizers
D. Wetting agents

D. Wetting agents

Slide 11: “SAA with balanced hydrophilic/lipophilic character (HLB 7-9) tend to be used as wetting agents.”

Middle of the scale = wetting and spreading agents.

Question 21 of 46
Highly hydrophilic SAA (HLB > 13) are used as:
A. Detergents and solubilizers (above CMC)
B. Antifoaming agents
C. W/O emulsifiers
D. Wetting agents only

A. Detergents and solubilizers (above CMC)

Slide 11/12: “Highly hydrophilic SAA (HLB >13) are used as detergents & solubilizers (above CMC).”

Top of the scale = cleaning and dissolving.

Question 22 of 46
In micellar solubilization, the SAA concentration should be:
A. Equal to half the CMC
B. Above the CMC
C. Below the CMC
D. Exactly zero

B. Above the CMC

Slide 12: “Micellar solubilization: SAA concentration should be above the CMC to ensure it is mainly existing in the bulk of solution not only on the liquid surface & to ensure complete surface coverage of the hydrophobic drug.”

Micelles are needed to hold the drug — and micelles only exist above the CMC.

Question 23 of 46
What is the application of antifoaming agents in pharmaceutical manufacture?
A. To act as antibacterials
B. To increase the surface tension of every product to 100 mN/m
C. To produce more foam in shampoos
D. To prevent or break foam formed during processing (e.g. mixing, stirring, filling), so manufacturing proceeds smoothly

D. To prevent or break foam formed during processing (e.g. mixing, stirring, filling), so manufacturing proceeds smoothly

Slide 14 asks: “What is the application of anti-foaming agents in pharmaceutical manufacture?” Extremely lipophilic SAA (HLB < 3) destabilise foam (slide 14 figure: “Foam stabilization” vs “Defoam mechanism”), so they are used to prevent/break foam that forms during mixing, stirring or filling liquids. (The slide only asks this; the answer is not written on the slide.)

Foam traps air and makes mixing and filling difficult; antifoams pop the bubbles.

5. Types of SAA Based on Their Charge

7 questions

Question 24 of 46
SAA are classified according to the charge on their:
A. Micelle core
B. Hydrophilic head
C. Counter-ion only
D. Lipophilic tail

B. Hydrophilic head

Slide 17: “Depending on their charge characteristics (in the hydrophilic head) SAA molecules may be anionic, cationic, zwitterionic (amphoteric) or non-ionic.”

Look at the head: negative, positive, both, or none.

Question 25 of 46
Sodium lauryl sulphate is an example of which type of SAA?
A. Amphoteric
B. Anionic
C. Cationic
D. Non-ionic

B. Anionic

Slide 18 table: Anionic SAA — alkali sulphates, alkali sulphonates (Sodium Lauryl sulphate) — used as detergents, solubilizer.

Sulphate head = negative charge = anionic.

Question 26 of 46
Anionic SAA are commonly used as detergents because:
A. Their charge changes with pH
B. They have no charge
C. They kill bacteria by binding to the cell membrane
D. Their negatively charged head gives high washing power, they are inexpensive, effective and produce abundant foam

D. Their negatively charged head gives high washing power, they are inexpensive, effective and produce abundant foam

Slide 19: anionic surfactant detergents have a negatively charged hydrophilic head, giving them high washing power and excellent cleaning properties… widely used because they are inexpensive and effective, and they produce abundant foam. Examples: LAS and SLES.

Negative head = strong cleaner (laundry, dish soap, shampoo).

Question 27 of 46
Benzalkonium chloride and cetyl pyridinium chloride are:
A. Amphoteric SAA used in cosmetics
B. Cationic SAA (quaternary ammonium compounds) used as antibacterials
C. Anionic SAA used as detergents
D. Non-ionic SAA used as emulsifiers

B. Cationic SAA (quaternary ammonium compounds) used as antibacterials

Slide 18 table: Cationic SAA — quaternary ammonium compounds (benzalkonium Cl, cetyl pyridinium Cl) — use: Antibacterials.

Quats = positive = antibacterial.

Question 28 of 46
Cationic SAA are antibacterial because:
A. Their positive head interacts with the negatively charged bacterial membrane and the tail penetrates the inner membrane, disrupting it
B. They produce a lot of foam
C. They have no charge so they enter cells freely
D. They reduce the pH of the bacteria

A. Their positive head interacts with the negatively charged bacterial membrane and the tail penetrates the inner membrane, disrupting it

Slide 20: “Cationic surfactants are antibacterial because their positive charge electrostatically interacts with negatively charged bacterial membranes… the head group's charge disrupts the outer membrane, and the hydrophobic tail penetrates the inner membrane, causing it to disintegrate.”

Positive attracts negative → the membrane is torn apart → the bacterium dies.

Question 29 of 46
N-dodecyl alanine and betaines are amphoteric SAA. Their main feature is:
A. They are used as antifoaming agents
B. Their charge changes according to pH changes; used in cosmetics
C. They are always negatively charged
D. They have no charge at any pH

B. Their charge changes according to pH changes; used in cosmetics

Slide 18 table: Amphoteric SAA — N-dodecyl alanine, betaines — use: Charge changes according to pH changes, cosmetics.

Amphoteric (zwitterionic) = can be + or − depending on pH.

Question 30 of 46
Spans and Tweens belong to which class of SAA?
A. Anionic
B. Cationic
C. Non-ionic
D. Amphoteric

C. Non-ionic

Slide 18 table: Non-ionic SAA — Sorbitan esters (Spans) low HLB; Polysorbates (Tweens) high HLB.

No charge in the head = non-ionic.

6. Poloxamers

5 questions

Question 31 of 46
Poloxamers (Pluronics) consist of:
A. One hydrophilic head and two lipophilic tails
B. Three blocks: two hydrophilic blocks and a central hydrophobic block
C. A sorbitan ester of a fatty acid
D. Two hydrophobic blocks and a central hydrophilic block

B. Three blocks: two hydrophilic blocks and a central hydrophobic block

Slide 22: “Poloxamers are amphiphilic molecules consisting of three blocks: two hydrophilic blocks, and a central hydrophobic block.”

Hydrophilic – hydrophobic – hydrophilic (PEO–PPO–PEO).

Question 32 of 46
In the poloxamer structure, increasing “x” (the hydrophilic outer parts) makes the poloxamer:
A. Gel at a lower temperature
B. More insoluble in water
C. Cationic
D. More soluble in water

D. More soluble in water

Slide 22: ““x” is the number of hydrophilic parts on the outside… Increasing “x” makes the poloxamer more soluble in water, while increasing “y” makes it more insoluble and helps it gel at a lower temperature.”

More water-loving blocks → dissolves better in water.

Question 33 of 46
Poloxamers are suitable for applications requiring thermal gelation because:
A. They melt into a liquid when heated
B. Their charge changes with pH
C. They are in a sol state at low temperature/concentration and turn into a gel at higher temperature/concentration
D. They are always solid

C. They are in a sol state at low temperature/concentration and turn into a gel at higher temperature/concentration

Slide 23: “Poloxamers can exist in a sol state at low temperatures and concentrations but transition into a gel state at higher concentrations and temperatures. The temperature-sensitive nature makes poloxamers suitable for applications requiring thermal gelation.”

Cold = liquid, warm (e.g. body temperature) = gel.

Question 34 of 46
In the figure, unimers form micelles when T > CMT, then the micelles pack into a gel when:
A. The pH is lowered
B. Water evaporates completely
C. T > LCST
D. T falls below CMT

C. T > LCST

Slide 23 figure: Unimers → (T > CMT) → Micelles → (T > LCST) → Micelle packing (gelation). Lowering T (↓T) reverses each step.

Heat step 1 = micelles; heat more = packed micelles = gel.

Question 35 of 46
Which is NOT listed as a pharmaceutical application of poloxamers?
A. Antifoaming agents
B. Eye drops
C. Injectable hydrogels
D. Wound dressing

A. Antifoaming agents

Slide 24 lists: injectable hydrogels, eye drops, wound dressing, emulsifying agents, solubilizing agents. Antifoaming is not listed (antifoams are extremely lipophilic SAA, HLB < 3).

Poloxamers = gels, eye drops, dressings, emulsifiers, solubilizers.

7. Spans & Tweens

7 questions

Question 36 of 46
Spans are:
A. Polyoxyethylene sorbitan esters, more soluble in water, used for O/W emulsions
B. Three-block copolymers
C. Quaternary ammonium compounds
D. Sorbitan esters of fatty acids with a small hydrophilic head, more soluble in oil, used for W/O emulsions (low HLB)

D. Sorbitan esters of fatty acids with a small hydrophilic head, more soluble in oil, used for W/O emulsions (low HLB)

Slide 25: Spans are composed of sorbitan esters of fatty acids; small hydrophilic head and long hydrophobic tails so they are hydrophobic; more soluble in oil than in water → useful for W/O emulsions; low HLB.

Span = oil-loving = W/O.

Question 37 of 46
Tweens differ from Spans because Tweens are:
A. Modified by the addition of polyoxyethylene chains, making them more water soluble (high HLB, O/W)
B. Free of any fatty acid
C. Made from a different sugar than sorbitan
D. Cationic

A. Modified by the addition of polyoxyethylene chains, making them more water soluble (high HLB, O/W)

Slide 25: “Tweens are derived from the same sorbitan esters as spans… but with an important difference: they are modified by the addition of polyoxyethylene chains. They are more soluble in water, making them useful for creating oil in water emulsion (O/W). They have high HLB value.”

Tween = Span + water-loving chains = O/W.

Question 38 of 46
Span 20, Span 60 and Span 80 are made with which fatty acids, respectively?
A. Oleic, lauric, stearic
B. Lauric, stearic, oleic
C. Lauric, oleic, stearic
D. Stearic, oleic, lauric

B. Lauric, stearic, oleic

Slide 26/27: “Lauric acid (Span / Tween 20), Stearic acid (Span / Tween 60), Oleic acid (Span / Tween 80).”

20 = lauric, 60 = stearic, 80 = oleic — the same for Span and Tween.

Question 39 of 46
Different Tweens (e.g. Tween 20 and Tween 80) have:
A. The same hydrophilic head but different lipophilic tails
B. Different hydrophilic heads but the same tail
C. The same head and the same tail
D. No hydrophilic head

A. The same hydrophilic head but different lipophilic tails

Slide 26: “Different Tweens have the same hydrophilic head but different lipophilic tails. Tweens & Spans have different hydrophilic heads.”

The fatty acid tail changes; the polyoxyethylene-sorbitan head stays the same.

Question 40 of 46
According to the table, Span 80 (sorbitan oleate) has HLB 4.3. It is best used as:
A. A W/O emulsifier
B. An O/W emulsifier
C. A solubilizer
D. A detergent

A. A W/O emulsifier

Slide 27 table: Sorbitan oleate (Span 80) HLB 4.3. Slide 11: HLB 3-8 → W/O emulsifier.

4.3 sits in the 3–8 band.

Question 41 of 46
According to the table, which surfactant has the lowest HLB and would act as an antifoaming agent?
A. Polyoxyethylene sorbitan trioleate (Tween 85)
B. Sorbitan laurate (Span 20)
C. Sorbitan trioleate (Span 85)
D. Sorbitan stearate (Span 60)

C. Sorbitan trioleate (Span 85)

Slide 27 table: Span 85 HLB 1.8 is the only value < 3. Slide 11: HLB < 3 → antifoaming agents.

1.8 is the bottom of the table.

Question 42 of 46
Tween 20 (lauric) has HLB 16.7 while Tween 80 (oleic) has HLB 15.0. This shows that:
A. HLB depends only on the head group
B. Oleic acid is more hydrophilic than lauric acid
C. The choice of fatty acid affects the surfactant's HLB value
D. All Tweens have the same HLB

C. The choice of fatty acid affects the surfactant's HLB value

Slide 27: “The choice of fatty acid affects the surfactant’s HLB value.” Table: Tween 20 = 16.7, Tween 80 = 15.0 (same head, different tails).

Longer (C18) tail → slightly more lipophilic → lower HLB.

8. Biological Surfactants

4 questions

Question 43 of 46
The cell membrane acts as a biological SAA because it is composed of:
A. A single layer of proteins
B. Polyoxyethylene chains
C. A phospholipid bilayer; each phospholipid has a hydrophilic head and 2 lipophilic tails
D. Cholesterol only

C. A phospholipid bilayer; each phospholipid has a hydrophilic head and 2 lipophilic tails

Slide 29: “Cell membrane is composed of a phospholipid bilayer. Each phospholipid unit is composed of hydrophilic head and 2 lipophilic tails.”

Two layers: heads outside facing water, tails inside facing each other.

Question 44 of 46
How does the phospholipid bilayer of the cell membrane affect drug absorption/penetration?
A. Its lipophilic core favours penetration of drugs with enough lipophilicity, while highly polar/ionized drugs cross poorly
B. Only highly ionized drugs can cross it
C. It allows all drugs to pass equally
D. It blocks all lipophilic drugs

A. Its lipophilic core favours penetration of drugs with enough lipophilicity, while highly polar/ionized drugs cross poorly

Slide 29 asks: “How would this affect drug absorption/penetration?” The bilayer has a lipophilic (tail) interior between hydrophilic surfaces, so a drug must be lipophilic enough to dissolve through the tail region; very polar or ionized drugs penetrate poorly. (The slide only asks this; the answer is not written on the slide.)

To cross a fatty wall, a drug must be at least partly fat-loving.

Question 45 of 46
In the cell membrane, cholesterol is positioned so that:
A. It floats freely outside the membrane
B. It replaces all phospholipid heads
C. Its hydrophobic region is embedded alongside the fatty-acid chains and its hydroxyl group points towards water
D. Its hydroxyl group is buried in the centre of the bilayer

C. Its hydrophobic region is embedded alongside the fatty-acid chains and its hydroxyl group points towards water

Slide 30: cholesterol has one hydroxyl group attached to a rigid steroid tetracyclic moiety, terminating with a flexible hydrocarbon chain… the hydrophobic region is embedded alongside the fatty-acid chains of lipids and the hydroxyl group points towards the water molecules surrounding the membrane.

OH = small polar head (towards water); steroid rings + chain = tail (in the bilayer).

Question 46 of 46
Lung surfactant is a lipoprotein complex at the alveolar air–liquid interface. Its main role is to:
A. Form inverted micelles in the blood
B. Kill bacteria in the lungs
C. Increase surface tension to keep alveoli closed
D. Reduce surface tension so the lungs do not collapse when we breathe out

D. Reduce surface tension so the lungs do not collapse when we breathe out

Slide 31: lung surfactant is arranged with the hydrophilic head groups in the water and the hydrophobic tails facing towards the air. “They mainly reduce the surface tension. This ensures that our lungs do not collapse when we breathe out.”

Lower surface tension = alveoli stay open.
